--- Page 247 ---
Exercise 10.3

1. Find the values of sin 2α, cos 2α and
tan 2α, when:
(i) sin α = 3/5, where 0 < α < π/2
sin α = 3/5, 0 < α < π/2
i.e. α lies in quad I
∴ cos α = √1 - sin² α
= √1 - (3/5)² = √1 - 9/25 = √25 - 9/25 = √16/25
cos α = 4/5 ∴ cos α is +ve in quad I
∴ sin 2α = 2 sin α cos α
= 2(3/5)(4/5)
sin 2α = 24/25
∴ cos 2α = 1 - 2 sin² α
= 1 - 2(3/5)² = 1 - 2(9/25) = 25 - 18/25
cos 2α = 7/25
∴ tan 2α = sin 2α / cos 2α = (24/25) / (7/25)
tan 2α = 24/7
(ii) cos α = 4/5, where 0 < α < π/2
cos α = 4/5, 0 < α < π/2
i.e. α lies in quad I
∴ sin α = √1 - cos² α
= √1 - (4/5)² = √1 - 16/25 = √25 - 16/25 = √9/25
sin α = 3/5 ∴ sin α is +ve in quad I
∴ sin 2α = 2 sin α cos α
= 2(3/5)(4/5)
sin 2α = 24/25
∴ cos 2α = 2 cos² α - 1
= 2(4/5)² - 1 = 2(16/25) - 1 = 32 - 25/25
cos 2α = 7/25
∴ tan 2α = sin 2α / cos 2α = (24/25) / (7/25)
tan 2α = 24/7

2. Prove the following identities:
(i) cot α - tan α = 2 cot 2α
L.H.S. = cot α - tan α
= cos α / sin α - sin α / cos α = (cos² α - sin² α) / (sin α cos α)
= cos 2α / (sin α cos α) = 2 cos 2α / (2 sin α cos α)
= 2 cos 2α / sin 2α = 2 cot 2α = R.H.S.
(ii) sin 2α / (1 + cos 2α) = tan α
L.H.S. = sin 2α / (1 + cos 2α)
= (2 sin α cos α) / (2 cos² α) = sin α / cos α
= tan α = R.H.S.
(iii) (1 - cos α) / sin α = tan α/2
L.H.S. = (1 - cos α) / sin α
= (2 sin² α/2) / (2 sin α/2 cos α/2) = sin α/2 / cos α/2
= tan α/2 = R.H.S.

--- Page 248 ---
(iv) $\frac{\cos \alpha - \sin \alpha}{\cos \alpha + \sin \alpha} = \sec 2\alpha - \tan 2\alpha$
R.H.S. $= \sec 2\alpha - \tan 2\alpha$
$= \frac{1}{\cos 2\alpha} - \frac{\sin 2\alpha}{\cos 2\alpha}$
$= \frac{1 - \sin 2\alpha}{\cos 2\alpha}$
$= \frac{\sin^2 \alpha + \cos^2 \alpha - 2\sin \alpha \cos \alpha}{\cos^2 \alpha - \sin^2 \alpha}$
$= \frac{(\cos \alpha - \sin \alpha)^2}{(\cos \alpha - \sin \alpha)(\cos \alpha + \sin \alpha)}$
$= \frac{\cos \alpha - \sin \alpha}{\cos \alpha + \sin \alpha} = \text{L.H.S.}$
Alternate Solution:
L.H.S. $= \frac{\cos \alpha - \sin \alpha}{\cos \alpha + \sin \alpha}$
$= \frac{\cos \alpha - \sin \alpha}{\cos \alpha + \sin \alpha} \times \frac{\cos \alpha - \sin \alpha}{\cos \alpha - \sin \alpha}$
$= \frac{(\cos \alpha - \sin \alpha)^2}{\cos^2 \alpha - \sin^2 \alpha}$
$= \frac{\cos^2 \alpha + \sin^2 \alpha - 2\sin \alpha \cos \alpha}{\cos 2\alpha}$
$= \frac{1 - \sin 2\alpha}{\cos 2\alpha}$
$= \frac{1}{\cos 2\alpha} - \frac{\sin 2\alpha}{\cos 2\alpha} = \sec 2\alpha - \tan 2\alpha$
$= \text{R.H.S.}$

(v) $\sqrt{\frac{1 + \sin \alpha}{1 - \sin \alpha}} = \frac{\sin \frac{\alpha}{2} + \cos \frac{\alpha}{2}}{\sin \frac{\alpha}{2} - \cos \frac{\alpha}{2}}$
L.H.S $= \sqrt{\frac{1 + \sin \alpha}{1 - \sin \alpha}}$
$= \sqrt{\frac{\sin^2 \frac{\alpha}{2} + \cos^2 \frac{\alpha}{2} + 2\sin \frac{\alpha}{2} \cos \frac{\alpha}{2}}{\sin^2 \frac{\alpha}{2} + \cos^2 \frac{\alpha}{2} - 2\sin \frac{\alpha}{2} \cos \frac{\alpha}{2}}}$
$= \sqrt{\frac{(\sin \frac{\alpha}{2} + \cos \frac{\alpha}{2})^2}{(\sin \frac{\alpha}{2} - \cos \frac{\alpha}{2})^2}} = \frac{\sin \frac{\alpha}{2} + \cos \frac{\alpha}{2}}{\sin \frac{\alpha}{2} - \cos \frac{\alpha}{2}}$
$= \text{R.H.S.}$

(vi) $\frac{\csc \theta + 2 \csc 2\theta}{\sec \theta} = \cot \frac{\theta}{2}$
L.H.S. $= \frac{\csc \theta + 2 \csc 2\theta}{\sec \theta}$
$= \frac{\csc \theta}{\sec \theta} + \frac{2 \csc 2\theta}{\sec \theta} = \frac{\cos \theta}{\sin \theta} + \frac{2}{\sin 2\theta} \cdot \cos \theta$
$= \frac{\cos \theta}{\sin \theta} + \frac{2 \cos \theta}{2 \sin \theta \cos \theta} = \frac{\cos \theta}{\sin \theta} + \frac{1}{\sin \theta}$
$= \frac{1 + \cos \theta}{\sin \theta} = \frac{2 \cos^2 \frac{\theta}{2}}{2 \sin \frac{\theta}{2} \cos \frac{\theta}{2}}$
$= \frac{\cos \frac{\theta}{2}}{\sin \frac{\theta}{2}} = \cot \frac{\theta}{2} = \text{R.H.S.}$

(vii) $1 + \tan \alpha \tan 2\alpha = \sec 2\alpha$
L.H.S. $= 1 + \tan \alpha \tan 2\alpha$
$= 1 + \frac{\sin \alpha}{\cos \alpha} \frac{\sin 2\alpha}{\cos 2\alpha}$
$= \frac{\cos 2\alpha \cos \alpha + \sin 2\alpha \sin \alpha}{\cos \alpha \cos 2\alpha}$
$= \frac{\cos(2\alpha - \alpha)}{\cos \alpha \cos 2\alpha} = \frac{\cos \alpha}{\cos \alpha \cos 2\alpha}$
$= \frac{1}{\cos 2\alpha} = \sec 2\alpha = \text{R.H.S.}$

(viii) $\frac{2 \sin \theta \sin 2\theta}{\cos \theta + \cos 3\theta} = \tan 2\theta \tan \theta$
L.H.S. $= \frac{2 \sin \theta \sin 2\theta}{\cos \theta + \cos 3\theta}$
$= \frac{2 \sin \theta \sin 2\theta}{\cos \theta + (4 \cos^3 \theta - 3 \cos \theta)}$
$= \frac{2 \sin \theta \sin 2\theta}{\cos \theta [1 + 4 \cos^2 \theta - 3]}$
$= \frac{2 \sin \theta \sin 2\theta}{\cos \theta (4 \cos^2 \theta - 2)}$
$= \frac{2 \sin \theta \sin 2\theta}{2 \cos \theta (2 \cos^2 \theta - 1)}$
$= \frac{2 \sin \theta \sin 2\theta}{2 \cos \theta \cos 2\theta} = \frac{\sin \theta}{\cos \theta} \frac{\sin 2\theta}{\cos 2\theta}$
$= \tan \theta \tan 2\theta = \text{R.H.S.}$

(xiv) $\frac{1+\sin 2\theta}{1-\sin 2\theta}=\tan^2\left(\frac{\pi}{4}+\theta\right)$
L.H.S $= \frac{1+\sin 2\theta}{1-\sin 2\theta}$
$= \frac{1+\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-2\theta\right)}{1-\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-2\theta\right)} \because \cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\theta\right)=\sin\theta$
$= \frac{2\cos^2\left(\frac{\frac{\pi}{2}-2\theta}{2}\right)}{2\sin^2\left(\frac{\frac{\pi}{2}-2\theta}{2}\right)}$
$= \cot^2\left(\frac{\pi}{4}-\theta\right) \because \tan\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\theta\right)=\cot\theta$
$= \tan^2\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\left(\frac{\pi}{4}-\theta\right)\right)$
$= \tan^2\left(\frac{\pi}{4}+\theta\right)$
(xv) $\cos^2\frac{\pi}{8}+\cos^2\frac{3\pi}{8}+\cos^2\frac{5\pi}{8}+\cos^2\frac{7\pi}{8}=2$
L.H.S
$= \cos^2\frac{\pi}{8}+\cos^2\frac{3\pi}{8}+\cos^2\frac{5\pi}{8}+\cos^2\frac{7\pi}{8}$
$= \cos^2\frac{\pi}{8}+\left[\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{\pi}{8}\right)\right]^2+\left[\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}+\frac{\pi}{8}\right)\right]^2$
$+\left[\cos\left(\pi-\frac{\pi}{8}\right)\right]^2$
$= \cos^2\frac{\pi}{8}+\sin^2\frac{\pi}{8}+\sin^2\frac{\pi}{8}+\cos^2\frac{\pi}{8}$
$= 1+1=2=$ R.H.S
3. Show that: $2\cos\theta=\sqrt{2+\sqrt{2+2\cos 4\theta}}$
R.H.S $= \sqrt{2+\sqrt{2+2\cos 4\theta}}$
$= \sqrt{2+\sqrt{2(1+\cos 4\theta)}}$
$= \sqrt{2+\sqrt{2(2\cos^2 2\theta)}} = \sqrt{2+2\cos 2\theta}$
$= \sqrt{2(1+\cos 2\theta)} = \sqrt{2(2\cos^2 \theta)}$
$= 2\cos\theta=$ L.H.S
4. Reduce $\sin^4\theta$ to an expression involving only function of multiples of $\theta$ raised to the first power.
Here $\sin^4\theta=(\sin^2\theta)^2$
$= \left(\frac{1-\cos 2\theta}{2}\right)^2 = \frac{1}{4}(1-\cos 2\theta)^2$
$= \frac{1}{4}(1+\cos^2 2\theta-2\cos 2\theta)$
$= \frac{1}{4}\left(1+\frac{1+\cos 4\theta}{2}-2\cos 2\theta\right)$
$= \frac{1}{4}\left(1+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\cos 4\theta}{2}-2\cos 2\theta\right)$
$= \frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{3}{2}+\frac{\cos 4\theta}{2}-2\cos 2\theta\right)$
$= \frac{3}{8}+\frac{\cos 4\theta}{8}-\frac{2\cos 2\theta}{4}$
$= \frac{3+cos 4\theta-4\cos 2\theta}{8}$
$= \frac{3-4\cos 2\theta+\cos 4\theta}{8}$

\text{L.H.S.}

= \left(\frac{(\sqrt{5}+1)(\sqrt{5}-1)}{4 \times 4}\right)^2
= \left(\frac{(\sqrt{5})^2-(1)^2}{16}\right)^2 = \left(\frac{5-1}{16}\right)^2
= \left(\frac{4}{16}\right)^2 = \left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^2 = \frac{1}{16} = \text{R.H.S.}

\underline{\text{Express the Product as Sums or Differences}}
\begin{tabular}{|l|}
\hline
$2 \sin \alpha \cos \beta = \sin(\alpha+\beta) + \sin(\alpha-\beta)$ \\
$2 \cos \alpha \sin \beta = \sin(\alpha+\beta) - \sin(\alpha-\beta)$ \\
$2 \cos \alpha \cos \beta = \cos(\alpha+\beta) + \cos(\alpha-\beta)$ \\
$-2 \sin \alpha \sin \beta = \cos(\alpha+\beta) - \cos(\alpha-\beta)$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\begin{tabular}{|l|}
\hline
$\sin P + \sin Q = 2 \sin\left(\frac{P+Q}{2}\right) \cos\left(\frac{P-Q}{2}\right)$ \\
$\sin P - \sin Q = 2 \cos\left(\frac{P+Q}{2}\right) \sin\left(\frac{P-Q}{2}\right)$ \\
$\cos P + \cos Q = 2 \cos\left(\frac{P+Q}{2}\right) \cos\left(\frac{P-Q}{2}\right)$ \\
$\cos P - \cos Q = -2 \sin\left(\frac{P+Q}{2}\right) \sin\left(\frac{P-Q}{2}\right)$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

1. Express the following products as
sums or differences:
(i) 2sin 3θ cos θ
We know that
2sin α cos β = sin(α + β) + sin(α − β)
So 2sin 3θ cos θ
= sin(3θ + θ) + sin(3θ − θ)
= sin 4θ + sin 2θ
(ii) 2cos 5θ sin 3θ
We know that
2cos α sin β = sin(α + β) − sin(α − β)
So 2cos 5θ sin 3θ
= sin(5θ + 3θ) − sin(5θ − 3θ)
= sin 8θ − sin 2θ
(iii) sin 5θ cos 2θ
sin 5θ cos 2θ = 1/2 (2sin 5θ cos 2θ)
= 1/2 [sin(5θ + 2θ) + sin(5θ − 2θ)]
= 1/2 [sin 7θ + sin 3θ]
(iv) 2sin 7θ sin 2θ
2sin 7θ sin 2θ = −(−2sin 7θ sin 2θ)
= −[cos(7θ + 2θ) − cos(7θ − 2θ)]
= −[cos 9θ − cos 5θ] = cos 5θ − cos 9θ
(v) cos(x + y)sin(x − y)
cos(x + y)sin(x − y)
= 1/2 2cos(x + y)sin(x − y)
= 1/2 [sin(x + y + x − y) − sin(x + y − x + y)]
= 1/2 [sin 2x − sin 2y]
(vi) cos(2x + 30°)cos(2x − 30°)
cos(2x + 30°)cos(2x − 30°)
= 1/2 [2cos(2x + 30°)cos(2x − 30°)]
= 1/2 [cos(2x + 30° + 2x − 30°)
+ cos(2x + 30° − 2x + 30°)]
= 1/2 [cos 4x + cos 60°]
(vii) sin 12° sin 46°
sin 12° sin 46°
= −1/2 [−2sin 46° sin 12°]
= −1/2 [cos(46° + 12°) − cos(46° − 12°)]
= −1/2 [cos 58° − cos 34°]
= 1/2 [cos 34° − cos 58°]
(viii) sin(x + 45°) sin(x − 45°)
sin(x + 45°)sin(x − 45°)
= −1/2 [−2sin(x + 45°)sin(x − 45°)]
= −1/2 [cos(x + 45° + x − 45°)
− cos(x + 45° − x + 45°)]
= −1/2 [cos 2x − cos 90°]
= 1/2 [cos 90° − cos 2x]
2. Express the following sums or
differences as products;
(i) sin 5θ + sin 3θ
sin 5θ + sin θ
= 2sin(5θ + 3θ / 2) cos(5θ − 3θ / 2)
= 2sin 8θ / 2 cos 2θ / 2 = 2sin 4θ cos θ

--- Page 254 ---
(ii) sin 8θ - sin 4θ
sin 8θ - sin 4θ
= 2 cos(8θ + 4θ / 2) sin(8θ - 4θ / 2)
= 2 cos 12θ / 2 sin 4θ / 2 = 2 cos 6θ sin 2θ
(iii) cos 6θ + cos 3θ
cos 6θ + cos 3θ
= 2 cos 6θ + 3θ / 2 cos 6θ - 3θ / 2
= 2 cos 9θ / 2 cos 3θ / 2
(iv) cos 7θ - cos θ
cos 7θ - cos θ
= -2 sin 7θ + θ / 2 sin 7θ - θ / 2
= -2 sin 8θ / 2 sin 6θ / 2 = -2 sin 4θ sin 3θ
(v) cos 12° + cos 48°
cos 12° + cos 48°
= 2 cos 12° + 48° / 2 cos 12° - 48° / 2
= 2 cos 60° / 2 cos -36° / 2
= 2 cos 30° cos (-18°)
= 2 cos 30° cos 18°
(vi) sin (x + 30°) + sin (x - 30°)
sin (x + 30°) + sin (x - 30°)
= 2 sin (x + 30° + x - 30° / 2) cos (x + 30° - x + 30° / 2)
= 2 sin 2x / 2 cos 60° / 2 = 2 sin x cos 30°

3. Prove the following identities:
(i) sin 3x - sin x / cos x - cos 3x = cot 2x
L.H.S. = sin 3x - sin x / cos x - cos 3x
= 2 cos (3x + x / 2) sin (3x - x / 2) / -2 sin (x + 3x / 2) sin (x - 3x / 2)
= 2 cos 4x / 2 sin 2x / 2 / -2 sin 4x / 2 sin -2x / 2 = 2 cos 2x sin x / -2 sin 2x sin (-x)
= 2 cos 2x sin x / -2 sin 2x (-sin x) = 2 cos 2x sin x / +2 sin 2x sin x
= cot 2x = R.H.S.
(ii) sin 8x + sin 2x / cos 8x + cos 2x = tan 5x
L.H.S. = sin 8x + sin 2x / cos 8x + cos 2x
= 2 sin 8x + 2x / 2 cos 8x - 2x / 2 / 2 cos 8x + 2x / 2 cos 8x - 2x / 2
= 2 sin 10x / 2 cos 6x / 2 / 2 cos 10x / 2 cos 6x / 2 = 2 sin 5x cos 3x / 2 cos 5x cos 3x
= tan 5x = R.H.S.
(iii) sin A - sin B / sin A + sin B = tan (A - B / 2) cot (A + B / 2)
L.H.S. = sin A - sin B / sin A + sin B
= 2 cos A + B / 2 sin A - B / 2 / 2 sin A + B / 2 cos A - B / 2
= tan A - B / 2 cot A + B / 2 = R.H.S.

--- Page 255 ---
(iv) $\frac{\sin 80^\circ + \sin 40^\circ}{\cos 80^\circ + \cos 40^\circ} = \sqrt{3}$
L.H.S $= \frac{\sin 80^\circ + \sin 40^\circ}{\cos 80^\circ + \cos 40^\circ}$
$= \frac{2\sin\left(\frac{80^\circ + 40^\circ}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{80^\circ - 40^\circ}{2}\right)}{2\cos\left(\frac{80^\circ + 40^\circ}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{80^\circ - 40^\circ}{2}\right)}$
$= \frac{\sin\left(\frac{120^\circ}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{40^\circ}{2}\right)}{\cos\left(\frac{120^\circ}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{40^\circ}{2}\right)}$
$= \frac{\sin 60^\circ \cos 20^\circ}{\cos 60^\circ \cos 20^\circ} = \tan 60^\circ = \sqrt{3}$
4. Prove that:
(i) $\cos 15^\circ + \cos 105^\circ + \cos 195^\circ + \cos 285^\circ = 0$
L.H.S
$= \cos 15^\circ + \cos 105^\circ + \cos 195^\circ + \cos 285^\circ$
$= \cos 15^\circ + \cos(90^\circ + 15^\circ) + \cos(180^\circ + 15^\circ)$
$+ \cos(270^\circ + 15^\circ)$
$= \cos 15^\circ - \sin 15^\circ - \cos 15^\circ + \sin 15^\circ$
$= 0 = \text{R.H.S}$
(ii) $\frac{\sin 2\theta + \sin 4\theta + \sin 6\theta + \sin 8\theta}{\cos 2\theta + \cos 4\theta + \cos 6\theta + \cos 8\theta} = \tan 5\theta$
L.H.S $= \frac{\sin 2\theta + \sin 4\theta + \sin 6\theta + \sin 8\theta}{\cos 2\theta + \cos 4\theta + \cos 6\theta + \cos 8\theta}$
$= \frac{[\sin 8\theta + \sin 2\theta] + [\sin 6\theta + \sin 4\theta]}{[\cos 8\theta + \cos 2\theta] + [\cos 6\theta + \cos 4\theta]}$
$= \frac{2\sin\left(\frac{8\theta + 2\theta}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{8\theta - 2\theta}{2}\right) + 2\sin\left(\frac{6\theta + 4\theta}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{6\theta - 4\theta}{2}\right)}{2\cos\left(\frac{8\theta + 2\theta}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{8\theta - 2\theta}{2}\right) + 2\cos\left(\frac{6\theta + 4\theta}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{6\theta - 4\theta}{2}\right)}$
$= \frac{2\sin 5\theta \cos 3\theta + 2\sin 5\theta \cos \theta}{2\cos 5\theta \cos 3\theta + 2\cos 5\theta \cos \theta}$
$= \frac{2\sin 5\theta (\cos 3\theta + \cos \theta)}{2\cos 5\theta (\cos 3\theta + \cos \theta)}$
$= \tan 5\theta = \text{R.H.S}$

(iii) $\cos^2\left(\frac{\pi}{4} - \frac{\alpha}{2}\right) - \cos^2\left(\frac{\pi}{4} + \frac{\alpha}{2}\right) = \sin \alpha$
L.H.S $= \cos^2\left(\frac{\pi}{4} - \frac{\alpha}{2}\right) - \cos^2\left(\frac{\pi}{4} + \frac{\alpha}{2}\right)$
$= \cos^2\left(\frac{\pi}{4} - \frac{\alpha}{2}\right) - \sin^2\left(\frac{\pi}{2} - \left(\frac{\pi}{4} + \frac{\alpha}{2}\right)\right)$
$\because \sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2} - \theta\right) = \cos \theta$
$= \cos^2\left(\frac{\pi}{4} - \frac{\alpha}{2}\right) - \sin^2\left(\frac{\pi}{4} - \frac{\alpha}{2}\right)$
$= \cos 2\left(\frac{\pi}{4} - \frac{\alpha}{2}\right) = \cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2} - \alpha\right)$
$= \sin \alpha = \text{R.H.S}$
(iv) $\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{4} - \theta\right)\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{4} + \theta\right) = \frac{1}{2}\cos 2\theta$
L.H.S. $= \sin\left(\frac{\pi}{4} - \theta\right)\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{4} + \theta\right)$
$= -\frac{1}{2}\left[-2\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{4} - \theta\right)\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{4} + \theta\right)\right]$
$= -\frac{1}{2}\left[\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{4} - \theta + \frac{\pi}{4} + \theta\right) - \cos\left(\frac{\pi}{4} - \theta - \frac{\pi}{4} - \theta\right)\right]$
$= -\frac{1}{2}\left[\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right) - \cos(-2\theta)\right]$
$= -\frac{1}{2}[0 - \cos 2\theta] = \frac{1}{2}\cos 2\theta = \text{R.H.S}$

(v) $\frac{\sin \theta + \sin 3\theta + \sin 5\theta + \sin 7\theta}{\cos \theta + \cos 3\theta + \cos 5\theta + \cos 7\theta} = \tan 4\theta$
L.H.S $= \frac{\sin \theta + \sin 3\theta + \sin 5\theta + \sin 7\theta}{\cos \theta + \cos 3\theta + \cos 5\theta + \cos 7\theta}$

$= \frac{(\sin 7\theta + \sin \theta) + (\sin 5\theta + \sin 3\theta)}{(\cos 7\theta + \cos \theta) + (\cos 5\theta + \cos 3\theta)}$

$= \frac{2\sin\left(\frac{7\theta + \theta}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{7\theta - \theta}{2}\right) + 2\sin\left(\frac{5\theta + 3\theta}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{5\theta - 3\theta}{2}\right)}{2\cos\left(\frac{7\theta + \theta}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{7\theta - \theta}{2}\right) + 2\cos\left(\frac{5\theta + 3\theta}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{5\theta - 3\theta}{2}\right)}$

$= \frac{2\sin\left(\frac{8\theta}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{6\theta}{2}\right) + 2\sin\left(\frac{8\theta}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{2\theta}{2}\right)}{2\cos\left(\frac{8\theta}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{6\theta}{2}\right) + 2\cos\left(\frac{8\theta}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{2\theta}{2}\right)}$

$= \frac{2\sin 4\theta \cos 3\theta + 2\sin 4\theta \cos \theta}{2\cos 4\theta \cos 3\theta + 2\cos 4\theta \cos \theta}$

$= \frac{2\sin 4\theta [\cos 3\theta + \cos \theta]}{2\cos 4\theta [\cos 3\theta + \cos \theta]} = \frac{\sin 4\theta}{\cos 4\theta} = \tan 4\theta = \text{R.H.S.}$

5. Prove that:
(i) $\cos 20^\circ \cos 40^\circ \cos 60^\circ \cos 80^\circ = \frac{1}{16}$
L.H.S. $= \cos 20^\circ \cos 40^\circ \cos 60^\circ \cos 80^\circ$
$= \frac{1}{2} \cos 80^\circ \cos 40^\circ \cos 20^\circ = \frac{1}{4} \{2 \cos 80^\circ \cos 40^\circ\} \cos 20^\circ$
$= \frac{1}{4} \{\cos(80^\circ + 40^\circ) + \cos(80^\circ - 40^\circ)\} \cos 20^\circ$
$= \frac{1}{4} \{\cos 120^\circ + \cos 40^\circ\} \cos 20^\circ = \frac{1}{4} \{\frac{-1}{2} + \cos 40^\circ\} \cos 20^\circ$
$= \frac{-1}{8} \cos 20^\circ + \frac{1}{4} \cos 40^\circ \cos 20^\circ$
$= \frac{-1}{8} \cos 20^\circ + \frac{1}{8} (2 \cos 40^\circ \cos 20^\circ)$
$= \frac{-1}{8} \cos 20^\circ + \frac{1}{8} [\cos(40^\circ + 20^\circ) + \cos(40^\circ - 20^\circ)]$
$= \frac{-1}{8} \cos 20^\circ + \frac{1}{8} (\cos 60^\circ + \cos 20^\circ) = \frac{-1}{8} \cos 20^\circ + \frac{1}{8} (\frac{1}{2} + \cos 20^\circ)$
$= \frac{-1}{8} \cos 20^\circ + \frac{1}{16} + \frac{1}{8} \cos 20^\circ = \frac{1}{16} = \text{R.H.S.}$

[REPORT ANY MISTAKE AT: FREEILM786@GMAIL.COM]                                                     [WEBSITE: WWW.FREEILM.COM]

--- Page 257 ---
(ii) $\sin \frac{\pi}{9} \sin \frac{2\pi}{9} \sin \frac{\pi}{3} \sin \frac{4\pi}{9} = \frac{3}{16}$
L.H.S. $= \sin 20^\circ \sin 40^\circ \sin 60^\circ \sin 80^\circ$
$= \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} [\sin 80^\circ \sin 40^\circ] \sin 20^\circ = -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4} [-2 \sin 80^\circ \sin 40^\circ] \sin 20^\circ$
$= -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4} [\cos(80^\circ + 40^\circ) - \cos(80^\circ - 40^\circ)] \sin 20^\circ$
$= -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4} [\cos 120^\circ - \cos 40^\circ] \sin 20^\circ = -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4} [\frac{-1}{2} - \cos 40^\circ] \sin 20^\circ$
$= \frac{\sqrt{3}}{8} \sin 20^\circ + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{4} \cos 40^\circ \sin 20^\circ$
$= \frac{\sqrt{3}}{8} \sin 20^\circ + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{8} (2 \cos 40^\circ \sin 20^\circ)$
$= \frac{\sqrt{3}}{8} \sin 20^\circ + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{8} [\sin(40^\circ + 20^\circ) - \sin(40^\circ - 20^\circ)]$
$= \frac{\sqrt{3}}{8} \sin 20^\circ + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{8} (\sin 60^\circ - \sin 20^\circ)$
$= \frac{\sqrt{3}}{8} \sin 20^\circ + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{8} \left( \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} - \sin 20^\circ \right)$
$= \frac{\sqrt{3}}{8} \sin 20^\circ + \frac{3}{16} - \frac{\sqrt{3}}{8} \sin 20^\circ = \frac{3}{16} = \text{R.H.S}$

(iii) $\sin 10^\circ \sin 30^\circ \sin 50^\circ \sin 70^\circ = \frac{1}{16}$
L.H.S. $= \sin 10^\circ \sin 30^\circ \sin 50^\circ \sin 70^\circ$
$= \frac{1}{2} (\sin 70^\circ \sin 50^\circ) \sin 10^\circ = \frac{1}{-4} (-2 \sin 70^\circ \sin 50^\circ) \sin 10^\circ$
$= \frac{1}{-4} [\cos(70^\circ + 50^\circ) - \cos(70^\circ - 50^\circ)] \sin 10^\circ$
$= \frac{-1}{4} [\cos 120^\circ - \cos 20^\circ] \sin 10^\circ = \frac{-1}{4} [\frac{-1}{2} - \cos 20^\circ] \sin 10^\circ$
$= \frac{1}{8} \sin 10^\circ + \frac{1}{4} \cos 20^\circ \sin 10^\circ$
$= \frac{1}{8} \sin 10^\circ + \frac{1}{8} (2 \cos 20^\circ \sin 10^\circ)$
$= \frac{1}{8} \sin 10^\circ + \frac{1}{8} [\sin(20^\circ + 10^\circ) - \sin(20^\circ - 10^\circ)]$
$= \frac{1}{8} \sin 10^\circ + \frac{1}{8} (\sin 30^\circ - \sin 10^\circ)$
$= \frac{1}{8} \sin 10^\circ + \frac{1}{8} \left( \frac{1}{2} - \sin 10^\circ \right)$
$= \frac{1}{8} \sin 10^\circ + \frac{1}{16} - \frac{1}{8} \sin 10^\circ = \frac{1}{16} = \text{R.H.S.}$

--- Page 258 ---
6. Prove that $\frac{\sin 3\theta}{1+2\cos 2\theta} = \sin \theta$; deduce
the value of $\sin 15^\circ$.
L.H.S $= \frac{\sin 3\theta}{1+2\cos 2\theta}$
$= \frac{3\sin \theta - 4\sin^3 \theta}{1+2[1-2\sin^2 \theta]}$
$= \frac{\sin \theta [3-4\sin^2 \theta]}{1+2-4\sin^2 \theta}$
$= \frac{\sin \theta (3-4\sin^2 \theta)}{(3-4\sin^2 \theta)}$
$= \sin \theta = \text{R.H.S}$
Now we deduce the value of $\sin 15^\circ$
As $\sin \theta = \frac{\sin 3\theta}{1+2\cos 2\theta}$
Put $\theta = 15^\circ$
$\sin 15^\circ = \frac{\sin 3(15^\circ)}{1+2\cos 30^\circ}$
$= \frac{\sin 45^\circ}{1+2\cos 30^\circ} = \frac{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}{1+2\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right)}$
$= \frac{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}{1+\sqrt{3}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}(\sqrt{3}+1)}$
$= \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}(\sqrt{3}+1)} \cdot \frac{(\sqrt{3}-1)}{(\sqrt{3}-1)} = \frac{\sqrt{3}-1}{\sqrt{2}[(\sqrt{3})^2-(1)^2]}$
$\sin 15^\circ = \frac{\sqrt{3}-1}{2\sqrt{2}}$

9. Prove that: $\frac{\sin^2 \alpha - \sin^2 \beta}{\sin \alpha \cos \alpha - \sin \beta \cos \beta} = \tan (\alpha + \beta)$
L.H.S $= \frac{\sin^2 \alpha - \sin^2 \beta}{\sin \alpha \cos \alpha - \sin \beta \cos \beta} = \frac{(\sin \alpha + \sin \beta)(\sin \alpha - \sin \beta)}{\frac{1}{2}[2\sin \alpha \cos \alpha - 2\sin \beta \cos \beta]}$
$= \frac{\left[2\sin\left(\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}\right)\right]\left[2\cos\left(\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}\right)\sin\left(\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}\right)\right]}{\frac{1}{2}[\sin 2\alpha - \sin 2\beta]}$

7. Prove that: $\tan 75^\circ - \tan 15^\circ = 2\sqrt{3}$
L.H.S $= \tan 75^\circ - \tan 15^\circ$
$= \tan(90^\circ - 15^\circ) - \tan 15^\circ$
$= \cot 15^\circ - \tan 15^\circ = \frac{\cos 15^\circ}{\sin 15^\circ} - \frac{\sin 15^\circ}{\cos 15^\circ}$
$= \frac{\cos^2 15^\circ - \sin^2 15^\circ}{\sin 15^\circ \cos 15^\circ} = \frac{\cos 2(15^\circ)}{\sin 15^\circ \cos 15^\circ}$
$= \frac{2\cos 30^\circ}{2\sin 15^\circ \cos 15^\circ} = \frac{2\cos 30^\circ}{\sin 30^\circ}$
$= \frac{2\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right)}{\frac{1}{2}} = 2\sqrt{3} = \text{R.H.S}$

8. Prove that: $\cos 15^\circ - \sin 15^\circ = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$
L.H.S $= \cos 15^\circ - \sin 15^\circ$
$= \cos(90^\circ - 75^\circ) - \sin 15^\circ$
$= \sin 75^\circ - \sin 15^\circ$
$= 2\cos\left(\frac{75^\circ+15^\circ}{2}\right)\sin\left(\frac{75^\circ-15^\circ}{2}\right)$
$= 2\cos 45^\circ \sin 30^\circ$
$= 2\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \frac{1}{2} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} = \text{R.H.S}$

Exercise 11.1
Q.1 Determine whether the following
functions are even, odd or neither odd
nor even.
(i) sin² x
Let f(x) = sin² x
Replace x by -x
f(-x) = [sin(-x)]²
f(-x) = [-sin x]²
sin(-0) = -sin 0
f(-x) = sin² x
=> f(-x) = f(x)
So, f(x) is an even function.
(ii) sin x + cos x
Let f(x) = sin x + cos x
Replace x by -x
f(-x) = sin(-x) + cos(-x)
f(-x) = -sin x + cos x
sin(-0) = -sin 0
cos(-0) = cos 0
f(-x) = -[sin x - cos x]
=> f(-x) ≠ f(x) and
f(-x) ≠ -f(x)
So, f(x) is neither even nor odd
function
(iii) sin⁴ x + cos⁴ x
Let f(x) = sin⁴ x + cos⁴ x
Replace x by -x
f(-x) = [sin(-x)]⁴ + [cos(-x)]⁴
f(-x) = [-sin x]⁴ + [cos x]⁴
sin(-0) = -sin 0
cos(-0) = cos 0
f(-x) = sin⁴ x + cos⁴ x
=> f(-x) = f(x)
So, f(x) is an even function
(iv) tan x + sec x
Let f(x) = tan x + sec x
Replace x by -x
f(-x) = tan(-x) + sec(-x)
f(-x) = -tan x + sec x
tan(-0) = -tan 0
sec(-0) = sec 0
f(-x) = -[tan x - sec x]
=> f(-x) ≠ f(x) and
f(-x) ≠ -f(x)
So, f(x) is neither even nor odd
function
(v) 1
cosec³ x
Let f(x) = 1
cosec³ x
Replace x by -x
f(-x) = 1
[cosec(-x)]³
cosec(-0) = -cosec 0
f(-x) = 1
[-cosec x]³
f(-x) = - 1
cosec³ x
=> f(-x) = -f(x)
So, f(x) is an odd function
(vi) sin x + sin 3x
cos x + cos 3x
Let f(x) = sin x + sin 3x
cos x + cos 3x
Replace x by -x
f(-x) = sin(-x) + sin 3(-x)
cos(-x) + cos 3(-x)
f(-x) = -sin x - sin 3x
cos x + cos 3x
WEBSITE: WWW.FREEILM.COM
REPORT ANY MISTAKE AT: FREEILM786@GMAIL.COM
WEBSITE: WWW.FREEILM.COM

--- Page 261 ---
$\because \sin(-\theta) = -\sin\theta$
$\because \cos(-\theta) = \cos\theta$
$f(-x) = -\left[ \frac{\sin x + \sin 3x}{\cos x + \cos 3x} \right]$
$\Rightarrow f(-x) = -f(x)$
So, $f(x)$ is an odd function.
(vii) $\frac{1}{\sec x + \sec^3 x}$
Let $f(x) = \frac{1}{\sec x + \sec^3 x}$
Replace $x$ by $-x$
$f(-x) = \frac{1}{\sec(-x) + [\sec(-x)]^3}$
$f(-x) = \frac{1}{\sec x + \sec^3 x}$
$\because \sec(-\theta) = \sec\theta$
$\Rightarrow f(-x) = f(x)$
So, $f(x)$ is an even function
(viii) $\frac{1}{\sec x + \cot^2 x}$
Let $f(x) = \frac{1}{\sec x + \cot^2 x}$
Replace $x$ by $-x$
$f(-x) = \frac{1}{\sec(-x) + [\cot(-x)]^2}$
$f(-x) = \frac{1}{\sec x + [-\cot(x)]^2}$
$\because \sec(-\theta) = \sec\theta$
$\because \cot(-\theta) = -\cot\theta$
$f(-x) = \frac{1}{\sec x + \cot^2 x}$
$\Rightarrow f(-x) = f(x)$
So, $f(x)$ is an even function.

Q.2 Find the periods of the following
functions:
(i) $\sin 5x$
We know that the period of sine is $2\pi$
$\because \sin(5x + 2\pi) = \sin 5x$
$\Rightarrow \sin 5\left(x + \frac{2\pi}{5}\right) = \sin 5x$
It means that the value of $\sin 5x$ repeats
when $x$ is increased by $\frac{2\pi}{5}$.
Hence $\frac{2\pi}{5}$ is the period of $\sin 5x$
(ii) $\cos 7x$
We know that the period of cosine is $2\pi$
$\because \cos(7x + 2\pi) = \cos 7x$
$\Rightarrow \cos 7\left(x + \frac{2\pi}{7}\right) = \cos 7x$
It means that the value of $\cos 7x$ repeats
when $x$ is increased by $\frac{2\pi}{7}$
Hence $\frac{2\pi}{7}$ is the period of $\cos 7x$
(iii) $\tan 3x$
We know that the period of tangent is $\pi$.
$\because \tan(3x + \pi) = \tan 3x$
$\Rightarrow \tan 3\left(x + \frac{\pi}{3}\right) = \tan 3x$
It means that the value of $\tan 3x$ repeats
when $x$ is increased by $\frac{\pi}{3}$.
Hence $\frac{\pi}{3}$ is the period of $\tan 3x$

--- Page 262 ---
(iv) $\cot \frac{x}{2}$
We know that the period of cotangent is $\pi$.
$\therefore \cot \left(\frac{x}{2} + \pi\right) = \cot \frac{x}{2}$
$\Rightarrow \cot \frac{1}{2}(x + 2\pi) = \cot \frac{x}{2}$
It means that the value of $\cot \frac{x}{2}$ repeats
when $x$ is increased by $2\pi$.
Hence $2\pi$ is the period of $\cot \frac{x}{2}$.

(v) $19\sin \left(\frac{\pi}{20}x\right)$
We know that the period of sine is $2\pi$.
$\therefore 19\sin \left(\frac{\pi}{20}x + 2\pi\right) = 19\sin \left(\frac{\pi}{20}x\right)$
$\Rightarrow 19\sin \frac{\pi}{20}(x + 40) = 19\sin \left(\frac{\pi}{20}x\right)$
It means that the value of $19\sin \left(\frac{\pi}{20}x\right)$
repeats when $x$ is increased by 40.
Hence 40 is the period of $19\sin \left(\frac{\pi}{20}x\right)$.

(vi) $\text{cosec} \left(\frac{2x}{5}\right)$
We know that the period of cosecant is $2\pi$.
$\therefore \text{cosec} \left(\frac{2x}{5} + 2\pi\right) = \text{cosec} \left(\frac{2x}{5}\right)$
$\Rightarrow \text{cosec} \frac{2}{5}(x + 5\pi) = \text{cosec} \left(\frac{2x}{5}\right)$
It means that the value of $\text{cosec} \left(\frac{2x}{5}\right)$
repeats when $x$ is increased by $5\pi$.
Hence $5\pi$ is the period of $\text{cosec} \left(\frac{2x}{5}\right)$.

(vii) $\frac{1}{2}\sin \left(\frac{3x}{2} - \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$
We know that the period of sine is $2\pi$
$\frac{1}{2}\sin \left[\left(\frac{3x}{2} + 2\pi\right) - \frac{\pi}{2}\right] = \frac{1}{2}\sin \left(\frac{3x}{2} - \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$
$\Rightarrow \frac{1}{2}\sin \left[\frac{3}{2}\left(x + \frac{4\pi}{3}\right) - \frac{\pi}{2}\right] = \frac{1}{2}\sin \left(\frac{3x}{2} - \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$
It means that the value of $\frac{1}{2}\sin \left(\frac{3x}{2} - \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$
repeats when $x$ is increased by $\frac{4\pi}{3}$.
Hence $\frac{4\pi}{3}$ is the period of
$\frac{1}{2}\sin \left(\frac{3x}{2} - \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$.

Alternate method:
$\frac{1}{2}\sin \left(\frac{3x}{2} - \frac{\pi}{2}\right) = \frac{1}{2}\sin \left[-\left(\frac{\pi}{2} - \frac{3x}{2}\right)\right]$
$\because \sin(-\theta) = -\sin\theta$
$\because \sin \left(\frac{\pi}{2} - \theta\right) = \cos\theta$
$= -\frac{1}{2}\cos \left(\frac{3x}{2}\right)$
We know that the period of cosine is $2\pi$.
$\therefore -\frac{1}{2}\cos \left(\frac{3x}{2} + 2\pi\right) = -\frac{1}{2}\cos \left(\frac{3x}{2}\right)$
$-\frac{1}{2}\cos \frac{3}{2}\left(x + \frac{4\pi}{3}\right) = -\frac{1}{2}\cos \left(\frac{3x}{2}\right)$
It means that the value of $-\frac{1}{2}\cos \left(\frac{3x}{2}\right)$
repeats when $x$ is increased by $\frac{4\pi}{3}$.
Hence $\frac{4\pi}{3}$ is the period of
$\frac{1}{2}\sin \left(\frac{3x}{2} - \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$.

--- Page 263 ---
(viii) $-5-3\sec\left(7\pi x+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$
To find the period of $-5-3\sec\left(7\pi x+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$,
consider only $-3\sec\left(7\pi x+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$
We know that the period of secant is $2\pi$.
$-3\sec\left(7\pi x+2\pi\right)+\frac{\pi}{4}=-3\sec\left(7\pi x+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$
$\Rightarrow-3\sec\left[7\pi\left(x+\frac{2}{7}\right)+\frac{\pi}{4}\right]=-3\sec\left(7\pi x+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$
It means that the value of
$-3\sec\left(7\pi x+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$ repeats when $x$ is
increased by $\frac{2}{7}$.
Hence $\frac{2}{7}$ is the period of
$-5-3\sec\left(7\pi x+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$
The addition of constant number $-5$ to
the secant function does not affect the
period.

(ix) $12+10\tan\left(\frac{\pi}{30}x\right)$
To find the period of $12+10\tan\left(\frac{\pi}{30}x\right)$,
consider only $10\tan\left(\frac{\pi}{30}x\right)$
We know that the period of tangent is $\pi$.
$10\tan\left(\frac{\pi}{30}x+\pi\right)=10\tan\left(\frac{\pi}{30}x\right)$
$\Rightarrow 10\tan\frac{\pi}{30}(x+30)=10\tan\left(\frac{\pi}{30}x\right)$
It means that the value of $10\tan\left(\frac{\pi}{30}x\right)$
repeats when $x$ is increased by 30.
Hence 30 is the period of
$12+10\tan\left(\frac{\pi}{30}x\right)$.
The addition of constant number 12 to
the tangent function does not affect the
period.

(x) $6-4\cot\left(\frac{7x}{4}+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$
To find the period of $6-4\cot\left(\frac{7x}{4}+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$,
consider only $-4\cot\left(\frac{7x}{4}+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$
We know that the period of cotangent is $\pi$.
$-4\cot\left(\frac{7x}{4}+\pi\right)+\frac{\pi}{4}=-4\cot\left(\frac{7x}{4}+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$
$\Rightarrow-4\cot\left[\frac{7}{4}\left(x+\frac{4\pi}{7}\right)+\frac{\pi}{4}\right]=-4\cot\left(\frac{7x}{4}+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$
It means that the value of $-4\cot\left(\frac{7x}{4}+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$
repeats when $x$ is increased by $\frac{4\pi}{7}$.
Hence $\frac{4\pi}{7}$ is the period of
$6-4\cot\left(\frac{7x}{4}+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$
The addition of constant number 6 to the
cotangent function does not affect the
period.

(xi) $9+30\sec\left(\frac{x}{15}+\frac{2\pi}{15}\right)$
To find the period of $9+30\sec\left(\frac{x}{15}+\frac{2\pi}{15}\right)$,
consider only $30\sec\left(\frac{x}{15}+\frac{2\pi}{15}\right)$
We know that the period of secant is $2\pi$.
$30\sec\left[\left(\frac{x}{15}+2\pi\right)+\frac{2\pi}{15}\right]=30\sec\left(\frac{x}{15}+\frac{2\pi}{15}\right)$
$\Rightarrow 30\sec\left[\frac{1}{15}(x+30\pi)+\frac{2\pi}{15}\right]=30\sec\left(\frac{x}{15}+\frac{2\pi}{15}\right)$
It means that the value of
$30\sec\left(\frac{x}{15}+\frac{2\pi}{15}\right)$ repeats when $x$ is
increase by $30\pi$. Hence $30\pi$ is the
period of $9+30\sec\left(\frac{x}{15}+\frac{2\pi}{15}\right)$. The
addition of constant number 9 to the
secant function does not affect the
period.

the curve y = sin x and the line y = x. The line and the curve intersect each other at x = 0. Hence

1. Find the maximum and minimum
values of the following functions:
(i) 3 - sin 3x
Let f(x) = 3 - sin 3x
The maximum value of f(x) will occur
when sin 3x = 1.
Here a = 3 and b = -1,
Maximum value of the function:
M = a + |b| = 3 + |-1| = 3 + 1 = 4
The minimum value of the function will
occur when sin 3x = -1
Minimum value of the function:
m = a - |b| = 3 - |-1| = 3 - 1 = 2
Thus, maximum value of the function is
4 and the minimum value is 2.

(ii) 3 + sin 2x
Let f(x) = 3 + sin 2x
The maximum value of f(x) will occur
when sin 2x = 1.
Here a = 3 and b = 1.
Maximum value of the function:
M = a + |b| = 3 + |1| = 3 + 1 = 4
The minimum value of the function will
occur when sin 2x = -1
Minimum value of the function:
m = a - |b| = 3 - |1| = 3 - 1 = 2
Thus, maximum value of the function is
4 and the minimum value is 2.

(iii) 1/2 + sin(5x + π)
Let f(x) = 1/2 + sin(5x + π)
The maximum value of f(x) will occur
when sin(5x + π) = 1.
Here a = 1/2 and b = 1.
Maximum value of the function:
M = a + |b| = 1/2 + |1| = 1/2 + 1 = 3/2
The minimum value of the function will
occur when sin(5x + π) = -1
Minimum value of the function:
m = a - |b| = 1/2 - 1 = -1/2
Thus, the maximum value of the
functions is 3/2 and minimum value of
the function is -1/2.

(iv) 3/2 + cos(x - π/4)
Let f(x) = 3/2 + cos(x - π/4)
The maximum value of f(x) will occur
when cos(x - π/4) = 1

--- Page 271 ---
Here $a=\frac{3}{2}$ and $b=1$
Maximum value of the function:
$M=a+|b|=\frac{3}{2}+1=\frac{5}{2}$
The minimum value of the function will
occur when $\cos\left(x-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)=-1$
Minimum value of the function:
$m=a-|b|=\frac{3}{2}-1=\frac{1}{2}$
Thus, maximum value of the function is $\frac{5}{2}$
and minimum value of the function is $\frac{1}{2}$.

(v) $1-3\cos 2x$
Let $f(x)=1-3\cos 2x$
The maximum value of $f(x)$ will occur
when $\cos 2x=1$
Here $a=1$ and $b=-3$
Maximum value of the function:
$M=a+|b|=1+|-3|=1+3=4$
The minimum value of the function will
occur when $\cos 2x=-1$
Minimum value of the function:
$m=a-|b|=1-|-3|=1-3=-2$
Thus, maximum value of the function is 4
and minimum value of the function is $-2$.

(vi) $1+2\sin\left(x+\frac{\pi}{6}\right)$
Let $f(x)=1+2\sin\left(x+\frac{\pi}{6}\right)$
The maximum value of $f(x)$ will occur
when $\sin\left(x+\frac{\pi}{6}\right)=1$
Here $a=1$ and $b=2$
Maximum value of the function:
$M=a+|b|=1+|2|=1+2=3$
The minimum value of the function will
occur when $\sin\left(x+\frac{\pi}{6}\right)=-1$
Minimum value of the function:
$m=a-|b|=1-|2|=1-2=-1$
Thus, maximum value of the function is 3
and minimum value of the function is $-1$.

(vii) $\frac{1}{10-2\sin 3x}$
Let $g(x)=\frac{1}{10-2\sin 3x}$
To find the maximum and minimum
values of $g(x)$, first we find the
maximum and minimum values of
$10-2\sin 3x$
So, consider $f(x)=10-2\sin 3x$
The maximum value of $f(x)$ will occur
when $\sin 3x=1$
Here $a=10$ and $b=-2$.
Maximum value of the function:
$M=a+|b|=10+|-2|=10+2=12$
The minimum value of the function will
occur when $\sin 3x=-1$
Minimum value of the function:
$m=a-|b|=10-|-2|=10-2=8$
Thus, maximum value of the function is
12 and the minimum value of the
function is 8.
Now, the reciprocal of the maximum
value is the minimum of $g(x)$ and the
reciprocal of the minimum value is the
maximum of $g(x)$.
Maximum value of $g(x)=\frac{1}{m}=\frac{1}{8}$
Minimum value of $g(x)=\frac{1}{M}=\frac{1}{12}$

(viii) $\frac{1}{7+3\cos(-2x)}$
Let $g(x)=\frac{1}{7+3\cos(-2x)}$
To find the maximum and minimum
values of $g(x)$, first we find the
maximum and minimum values of
$7+3\cos(-2x)$
So, consider $f(x)=7+3\cos(-2x)$
$\Rightarrow f(x)=7+3\cos(2x)$
$\because \cos(-\theta)=\cos\theta$
The maximum value of $f(x)$ will occur
when $\cos 2x=1$

(ix) 1 / (5 - 3cos(3x - 1))

2. The temperature T in a certain city
varies throughout the day according to
the equation
T(t) = 13/2 sin(π/6 t - π/9) + 15, where t is
the time in hours, with t = 0
corresponding to midnight.
(a) Find the maximum and minimum
temperature during the day.
(b) Find the temperature at t = 9 hours
(9 : 00 a.m.).
T(t) = 13/2 sin(π/6 t - π/9) + 15 (i)
(a) Find the maximum and minimum
temperature during the day.
For maximum temperature:
T_max = a + |b| where a = 15 and b = 13/2
So, T_max = 15 + |13/2| = 15 + 13/2 = 43/2
T_max = 21.5°
For minimum temperature:
T_min = a - |b| = 15 - |13/2| = 15 - 13/2 = 17/2
So, T_min = 8.5°
(b) Find the temperature at t = 9 hours
(9 : 00 a.m.).
T(t) = 13/2 sin(π/6 t - π/9) + 15 (i)
Put t = 9 in equation (i)
T(9) = 13/2 sin(π/6 * 9 - π/9) + 15
= 13/2 sin(3π/2 - π/9) + 15
= 13/2 [-cos(π/9)] + 15 = -13/2 cos(π/9) + 15
T(9) ≈ 8.89°
So, temperature at 9:00 a.m. is 8.89°
approximately.

5. A giant Ferris wheel has a diameter of
60 feet. The lowest point of the wheel is
located 6 feet above the ground. The
wheel completes one full revolution
every 80 seconds.
(a) Model an equation that represent the
height h(t) of a rider on the Ferris
wheel at any given time t.
(b) Find the maximum height of a rider.
(c) Find the height of the rider from the
ground after 35 seconds.
Since it takes 80 seconds for the Ferris
wheel to complete one full revolution
(one cycle), which is the period of the
Ferris wheel, so, period = 80.
2π

b = 60 = 30 ft) Radius = 60 = 2
The vertical shift "a" is the height of the
center of the Ferris wheel above the
ground.
Since the lowest point is 6 feet above the
ground. So, a = 6+b = 6+30 = 36.
We can model the height of a rider using
(sine or cosine), because it reflects the
periodic nature of the motion.
We usually choose a cosine function if
the rider starts at the maximum height.
Since the rider starts at the lowest point
and goes up, we can easily model the
required equation as a negative cosine
function.
So, h(t) = -bcos(ct) + a, where t is the
time and h is the height.

7. A carnival ride consists of a vertical
wheel with a diameter of 40 feet. The
centre of the wheel is 28 feet above the
ground. The wheel rotates at a
constant speed and takes 120 second to
make one complete revolution. Model
an equation that describes the height
h(t) of a rider on the wheel as a
function of time t. How high is the
rider from the ground after 90
seconds? At what times will the rider
be 36 feet above the ground?
The general form of a function is:
h(t) = -Acos(Bt - C) + D
The amplitude A is the radius of the wheel:
40
⇒ A = = 20
2

8. Suppose the temperature T in degrees
Fahrenheit of Lahore city in a month
of December throughout the day can
be modeled by the equation:
π
T = 64 + 8sin( (t - 8)), where t is
12
the time in hours. The temperature
oscillates 8 degrees above and below an
average temperature of 64 degrees.
(a) Find the temperature at t = 9 hours?
(b) At what time the temperature will be
maximum?
(c) Calculate the maximum temperature.
π
∴ T = 64 + 8sin( (t - 8)) (i)
12
Where "T" is the temperature in
degrees and "t" is the time in hours.

--- Page 276 ---
(a) Find the temperature at t = 9 hours?
Put t = 9 in equation (i) , we get:
T = 64 + 8sin(π/12 × (9-8))
T = 64 + 8sin(π/12)
T = 64 + 2.07
⇒ T = 66.07°F
(b) At what time the temperature will be
maximum?
For maximum temperature
= 64 + |8| = 64 + 8 = 72
So, put T = 72 in equation (i), we get:
72 = 64 + 8sin(π/12(t-8))
72 - 64 = 8sin(π/12(t-8))
8/8 = sin(π/12(t-8))
⇒ sin⁻¹(1) = π/12(t-8)
π/2 × 12/π = t-8
⇒ t = 14 hours or 2 p.m.
(c) Calculate the maximum temperature.
To calculate maximum temperature,
put sin(π/12(t-8)) = 1 in eq (i)
So, equation (i) becomes:
T = 64 + 8(1)
⇒ T = 72°F

9. Suppose the population of a coastal
city follows a sinusoidal pattern due to
seasonal migration. The population of
the city over the course of a year can
be modeled by the equation:
P(t) = 70000 + 10000cos(π/6 t - π/2), P(t)
is the population at time t (t is the time
in months, with t = 0 corresponding to
January 1st) where t denoted the
months in a year.
(a) Find the population of a city at t = 7
months
(b) Find the maximum population
The population is modeled by:
P(t) = 70000 + 10000cos(π/6 t - π/2) (i)
Where "t" is the time in months, with
t = 0 corresponding to January 1st
∴ The cosine function has a maximum
value of 1.
(a) Find the population of a city at t = 7
months
Put t = 7 into equation (i), we get:
P(7) = 70000 + 10000cos(π/6(7) - π/2)
P(7) = 70000 + 10000cos(7π/6 - 3π/6)
P(7) = 70000 + 10000cos(4π/6)
P(7) = 70000 + 10000cos(2π/3)
∴ cos(2π/3) = -1/2
P(7) = 70000 + 10000(-1/2)
P(7) = 70000 - 5000 = 65000
(b) Find the maximum population
The maximum value of cos(π/6 t - π/2) is 1.
So, we put the maximum value of cosine
into equation (i)
Pmax = 70000 + 10000(1)
⇒ Pmax = 80000

Exercise 12.1

Q.1 Find the limit of the following
sequences if exists:
(i) $a_n = \frac{2n+3}{n+1}$
$a_n = \frac{2n+3}{n+1}$
Applying limit $n \to \infty$ on both sides
$\lim_{n \to \infty} a_n = \lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{2n+3}{n+1}$
$= \lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{n(2+\frac{3}{n})}{n(1+\frac{1}{n})} = \lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{2+\frac{3}{n}}{1+\frac{1}{n}}$
As $n \to \infty, \frac{3}{n} \to 0, \frac{1}{n} \to 0$
$= \frac{2+0}{1+0} = \frac{2}{1}$
$\lim_{n \to \infty} a_n = 2$

(ii) $b_n = \frac{2n+3}{n^2+1}$
$b_n = \frac{2n+3}{n^2+1}$
Applying limit $n \to \infty$ on both sides
$\lim_{n \to \infty} b_n = \lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{2n+3}{n^2+1}$
$= \lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{n^2(\frac{2}{n}+\frac{3}{n^2})}{n^2(1+\frac{1}{n^2})} = \lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{\frac{2}{n}+\frac{3}{n^2}}{1+\frac{1}{n^2}}$
As $n \to \infty, \frac{2}{n} \to 0, \frac{3}{n^2} \to 0, \frac{1}{n^2} \to 0$
$= \frac{0+0}{1+0}$
$= \frac{0}{1} = 0$

(iii) $c_n = \frac{5n^2}{2n+3}$
$c_n = \frac{5n^2}{2n+3}$
Applying limit $n \to \infty$ on both sides
$\lim_{n \to \infty} c_n = \lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{5n^2}{2n+3}$
$= \lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{5n^2}{n^2(\frac{2}{n}+\frac{3}{n^2})} = \lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{5}{\frac{2}{n}+\frac{3}{n^2}}$
As $n \to \infty, \frac{2}{n} \to 0, \frac{3}{n^2} \to 0$
$= \frac{5}{0+0} = \frac{5}{0} = \infty$
Since, $\lim_{n \to \infty} c_n$ does not exist, so $\{c_n\}$ is
divergent sequence.

(iv) $d_n = \frac{n^2-3n+1}{2n^2+n+4}$
$d_n = \frac{n^2-3n+1}{2n^2+n+4}$
Applying limit $n \to \infty$ on both sides
$\lim_{n \to \infty} d_n = \lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{n^2-3n+1}{2n^2+n+4}$
$= \lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{n^2(1-\frac{3}{n}+\frac{1}{n^2})}{n^2(2+\frac{1}{n}+\frac{4}{n^2})} = \lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{1-\frac{3}{n}+\frac{1}{n^2}}{2+\frac{1}{n}+\frac{4}{n^2}}$
As $n \to \infty, \frac{3}{n} \to 0, \frac{1}{n} \to 0, \frac{1}{n^2} \to 0, \frac{4}{n^2} \to 0$
$\lim_{n \to \infty} d_n = \frac{1-0+0}{2+0+0} = \frac{1}{2}$

Q.2 Evaluate each limit by using theorems
of limits:
(i) $\lim_{x \to 3} (2x+4)$
$\lim_{x \to 3} (2x+4)$
$= \lim_{x \to 3} (2x) + \lim_{x \to 3} (4)$
$= 2 \lim_{x \to 3} (x) + 4 = 2(3) + 4 = 6 + 4 = 10$
$\lim_{x \to 3} (2x+4) = 10$

(ii) $\lim_{x \to 1} (3x^2 - 2x + 4)$
$\lim_{x \to 1} (3x^2 - 2x + 4)$
$= \lim_{x \to 1} (3x^2) - \lim_{x \to 1} (2x) + \lim_{x \to 1} (4)$
$= 3 \lim_{x \to 1} (x^2) - 2 \lim_{x \to 1} (x) + 4$
$= 3(1)^2 - 2(1) + 4 = 3 - 2 + 4 = 5$
$\lim_{x \to 1} (3x^2 - 2x + 4) = 5$

[REPORT ANY MISTAKE AT: FREEILM786@GMAIL.COM] [WEBSITE: WWW.FREEILM.COM]

--- Page 278 ---
(iii) $Lim_{x\to 3} \sqrt{x^2+x+4}$
$Lim_{x\to 3} \sqrt{x^2+x+4} = \sqrt{Lim_{x\to 3}(x^2+x+4)}$
$= \sqrt{Lim_{x\to 3}x^2 + Lim_{x\to 3}x + Lim_{x\to 3}4}$
$= \sqrt{3^2+3+4} = \sqrt{9+3+4} = \sqrt{16} = 4$
$Lim_{x\to 3} \sqrt{x^2+x+4} = 4$
(iv) $lim_{x\to 2} \sqrt{x^2+4}$
$= \sqrt{lim_{x\to 2}(x^2+4)} = \sqrt{lim_{x\to 2}(x^2)+lim_{x\to 2}(4)}$
$lim_{x\to 2} \sqrt{x^2+4} = \sqrt{2^2+4}$
$= \sqrt{8} = 2\sqrt{2}$
(v) $Lim_{x\to 2} (\sqrt{x^3+1}-\sqrt{x^2+5})$
$Lim_{x\to 2} (\sqrt{x^3+1}-\sqrt{x^2+5})$
$= \sqrt{Lim_{x\to 2}(x^3+1)} - \sqrt{Lim_{x\to 2}(x^2+5)}$
$= \sqrt{2^3+1} - \sqrt{2^2+5} = \sqrt{9} - \sqrt{9}$
$Lim_{x\to 2} (\sqrt{x^3+1}-\sqrt{x^2+5}) = 0$
(vi) $Lim_{x\to -2} \frac{2x^3+5x}{3x-2}$
$Lim_{x\to -2} \frac{2x^3+5x}{3x-2} = \frac{2Lim_{x\to -2}(x^3)+5Lim_{x\to -2}(x)}{3Lim_{x\to -2}(x)-Lim_{x\to -2}(2)}$
$= \frac{2(-2)^3+5(-2)}{3(-2)-2} = \frac{2(-8)+5(-2)}{3(-2)-2}$
$= \frac{-16-10}{-6-2} = \frac{-26}{-8} = \frac{13}{4}$

Q.3 Evaluate each limit by using algebraic
techniques.
(i) $Lim_{x\to -1} \frac{x^3-x}{x+1}$
$Lim_{x\to -1} \frac{x^3-x}{x+1}$
$\because a^2-b^2=(a+b)(a-b)$
$= Lim_{x\to -1} \frac{x(x^2-1)}{x+1} = Lim_{x\to -1} \frac{x(x-1)(x+1)}{x+1}$
$= Lim_{x\to -1} x(x-1) = -1(-1-1) = -1(-2) = 2$
(ii) $lim_{x\to 3} \left( \frac{x^2-5x+6}{x^2-2x-3} \right)$
$= lim_{x\to 3} \frac{x^2-5x+6}{x^2-2x-3} = lim_{x\to 3} \frac{x^2-3x-2x+6}{x^2-3x+x-3}$
$= lim_{x\to 3} \frac{x(x-3)-2(x-3)}{x(x-3)+1(x-3)}$
$= lim_{x\to 3} \frac{(x-2)(x-3)}{(x+1)(x-3)} = lim_{x\to 3} \frac{x-2}{x+1}$
$= \frac{3-2}{3+1} = \frac{1}{4}$
So, $lim_{x\to 3} \frac{x^2-5x+6}{x^2-2x-3} = \frac{1}{4}$
(iii) $lim_{x\to 2} \left( \frac{x^3-8}{x^2-5x+6} \right)$
$= lim_{x\to 2} \frac{x^3-8}{x^2-5x+6}$
$\because a^3-b^3=(a-b)(a^2+ab+b^2)$
$= lim_{x\to 2} \frac{x^3-2^3}{x^2-5x+6}$
$= lim_{x\to 2} \frac{(x-2)(x^2+2x+4)}{x^2-2x-3x+6}$
$= lim_{x\to 2} \frac{(x-2)(x^2+2x+4)}{x(x-2)-3(x-2)}$
$= lim_{x\to 2} \frac{(x-2)(x^2+2x+4)}{(x-3)(x-2)}$
$= lim_{x\to 2} \frac{x^2+2x+4}{x-3} = \frac{2^2+2(2)+4}{2-3} = \frac{4+4+4}{-1} = -12$
So, $lim_{x\to 2} \frac{x^3-8}{x^2-5x+6} = -12$

--- Page 279 ---
[WANT TO GET ALL SUBJECT NOTES: WWW.FREEILM.COM] [WEBSITE: WWW.FREEILM.COM]

(iv) $Lim_{x \to 1} \frac{x^3 - 3x^2 + 3x - 1}{x^3 - x}$
$Lim_{x \to 1} \frac{x^3 - 3x^2 + 3x - 1}{x^3 - x}$
$= Lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(x)^3 - 3(x)^2(1) + 3(x)(1)^2 - (1)^3}{x(x^2 - 1)}$
$= Lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(x-1)^3}{x(x^2 - 1)} = Lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(x-1)^3}{x(x-1)(x+1)}$
$= Lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(x-1)^2}{x(x+1)} = \frac{(1-1)^2}{1(1+1)} = \frac{0}{2} = 0$

(v) $\lim_{x \to 2} \left( \frac{x^3 - 6x^2 + 12x - 8}{x^3 - 4x} \right)$
$= \lim_{x \to 2} \frac{x^3 - 6x^2 + 12x - 8}{x^3 - 4x}$
$= \lim_{x \to 2} \frac{x^3 - 8 - 6x^2 + 12x}{x(x^2 - 4)}$
$= \lim_{x \to 2} \frac{x^3 - 2^3 - 6x(x - 2)}{x(x^2 - 4)}$
$= \lim_{x \to 2} \frac{x^3 - 2^3 - 6x(x - 2)}{x(x^2 - 2^2)}$
$= \lim_{x \to 2} \frac{(x-2)(x^2 + 2x + 4) - 6x(x - 2)}{x(x-2)(x+2)}$
$= \lim_{x \to 2} \frac{(x-2)(x^2 + 2x + 4 - 6x)}{x(x-2)(x+2)}$
$= \lim_{x \to 2} \frac{x^2 - 4x + 4}{x(x+2)} = \frac{2^2 - 4(2) + 4}{2(2+2)}$
$= \frac{4 - 8 + 4}{2(4)} = \frac{8 - 8}{8} = \frac{0}{8} = 0$
So, $\lim_{x \to 2} \frac{x^3 - 6x^2 + 12x - 8}{x^3 - 4x} = 0$

(vi) $\lim_{x \to 1} \left( \frac{x^4 - 1}{x^2 - 3x + 2} \right)$
$= \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{x^4 - 1}{x^2 - 3x + 2} = \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(x^2)^2 - (1)^2}{x^2 - 2x - x + 2}$
$\because a^2 - b^2 = (a+b)(a-b)$
$= \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(x^2 + 1)(x^2 - 1)}{x(x-2) - 1(x-2)}$
$= \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(x^2 + 1)(x^2 - 1^2)}{(x-1)(x-2)}$
$= \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(x^2 + 1)(x+1)(x-1)}{(x-1)(x-2)}$
$= \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(x^2 + 1)(x+1)}{x-2} = \frac{(1^2 + 1)(1+1)}{1-2}$
$= \frac{(1+1)(2)}{-1} = -(2)(2) = -4$
So, $\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{x^4 - 1}{x^2 - 3x + 2} = -4$

(vii) $\lim_{x \to 2} \frac{x - 2}{\sqrt{x+2} - \sqrt{6-x}}$
$= \lim_{x \to 2} \frac{x - 2}{\sqrt{x+2} - \sqrt{6-x}}$
Rationalizing by 'Denominator'
$= \lim_{x \to 2} \frac{x - 2}{\sqrt{x+2} - \sqrt{6-x}} \times \frac{\sqrt{x+2} + \sqrt{6-x}}{\sqrt{x+2} + \sqrt{6-x}}$
$= \lim_{x \to 2} \frac{(x-2)(\sqrt{x+2} + \sqrt{6-x})}{(\sqrt{x+2})^2 - (\sqrt{6-x})^2}$
$= \lim_{x \to 2} \frac{(x-2)(\sqrt{x+2} + \sqrt{6-x})}{x+2 - 6 + x}$
$= \lim_{x \to 2} \frac{(x-2)(\sqrt{x+2} + \sqrt{6-x})}{2x - 4}$
$= \lim_{x \to 2} \frac{(x-2)(\sqrt{x+2} + \sqrt{6-x})}{2(x-2)}$
$= \lim_{x \to 2} \frac{\sqrt{x+2} + \sqrt{6-x}}{2}$

[REPORT ANY MISTAKE AT: FREEILM786@GMAIL.COM] [WEBSITE: WWW.FREEILM.COM]

--- Page 280 ---
[WANT TO GET ALL SUBJECT NOTES: WWW.FREEILM.COM] [WEBSITE: WWW.FREEILM.COM]

= $\frac{\sqrt{2+2}+\sqrt{6-2}}{2} = \frac{\sqrt{4}+\sqrt{4}}{2}$
= $\frac{2+2}{2} = \frac{4}{2} = 2$
So, $\lim_{x\to 2} \frac{x-2}{\sqrt{x+2}-\sqrt{6-x}} = 2$
(viii) $\lim_{h\to 0} \frac{\sqrt{x+h}-\sqrt{x}}{h}$
$\lim_{h\to 0} \frac{\sqrt{x+h}-\sqrt{x}}{h}$
By rationalizing with numerator
= $\lim_{h\to 0} \frac{\sqrt{x+h}-\sqrt{x}}{h} \times \frac{\sqrt{x+h}+\sqrt{x}}{\sqrt{x+h}+\sqrt{x}}$
= $\lim_{h\to 0} \frac{(\sqrt{x+h})^2-(\sqrt{x})^2}{h[\sqrt{x+h}+\sqrt{x}]}$
= $\lim_{h\to 0} \frac{x+h-x}{h[\sqrt{x+h}+\sqrt{x}]} = \lim_{h\to 0} \frac{h}{h[\sqrt{x+h}+\sqrt{x}]}$
= $\lim_{h\to 0} \frac{1}{\sqrt{x+h}+\sqrt{x}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{x+0}+\sqrt{x}}$
= $\frac{1}{\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{x}} = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}$
(ix) $\lim_{x\to a} \frac{x^n-a^n}{x^m-a^m}$
$\lim_{x\to a} \frac{x^n-a^n}{x^m-a^m}$
= $\lim_{x\to a} \frac{(x-a)(x^{n-1}+ax^{n-2}+a^2x^{n-3}+...+a^{n-1})}{(x-a)(x^{m-1}+ax^{m-2}+a^2x^{m-3}+...+a^{m-1})}$
= $\lim_{x\to a} \frac{x^{n-1}+ax^{n-2}+a^2x^{n-3}+...+a^{n-1}}{x^{m-1}+ax^{m-2}+a^2x^{m-3}+...+a^{m-1}}$
= $\frac{a^{n-1}+aa^{n-2}+a^2a^{n-3}+...+a^{n-1}}{a^{m-1}+aa^{m-2}+a^2a^{m-3}+...+a^{m-1}}$
= $\frac{a^{n-1}+a^{n-1}+a^{n-1}+......+a^{n-1}}{a^{m-1}+a^{m-1}+a^{m-1}+......+a^{m-1}}$
= $\frac{na^{n-1}}{ma^{m-1}} = \frac{n}{m} a^{n-1-(m-1)} = \frac{n}{m} a^{n-1-m+1} = \frac{n}{m} a^{n-m}$
Q.4 Evaluate the following limits:
(i) $\lim_{x\to 0} \frac{\sin 5x}{x}$
$\lim_{x\to 0} \frac{\sin(5x)}{x}$

Multiply and divide by 5
= $\lim_{x\to 0} 5 \times \frac{\sin 5x}{5x} = 5 \lim_{x\to 0} \frac{\sin 5x}{5x}$
As $x\to 0$, then $5x\to 0$
= $5 \lim_{5x\to 0} \frac{\sin 5x}{5x} = 5(1) = 5$
(ii) $\lim_{x\to 0} \frac{\sin x^\circ}{x}$
$\lim_{x\to 0} \frac{\sin x^\circ}{x} = \lim_{x\to 0} \frac{\sin(\frac{\pi}{180}x)}{x}$
$\because x^\circ = \frac{\pi}{180}x$
Multiply and divide by $\frac{\pi}{180}$
= $\lim_{x\to 0} \frac{\pi}{180} \times \frac{\sin(\frac{\pi}{180}x)}{\frac{\pi}{180}x} = \frac{\pi}{180} \lim_{\frac{\pi}{180}x\to 0} \frac{\sin(\frac{\pi}{180}x)}{\frac{\pi}{180}x}$
When $x\to 0$, then $\frac{\pi}{180}x\to 0$
= $\frac{\pi}{180} \lim_{\frac{\pi}{180}x\to 0} \frac{\sin(\frac{\pi}{180}x)}{\frac{\pi}{180}x} = \frac{\pi}{180}(1) = \frac{\pi}{180}$
(iii) $\lim_{\theta\to 0} \frac{1-\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}$
$\lim_{\theta\to 0} \frac{1-\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}$
By rationalizing with numerator
= $\lim_{\theta\to 0} \frac{1-\cos\theta}{\sin\theta} \times \frac{1+\cos\theta}{1+\cos\theta}$
= $\lim_{\theta\to 0} \frac{1^2-\cos^2\theta}{\sin\theta(1+\cos\theta)} = \lim_{\theta\to 0} \frac{1-\cos^2\theta}{\sin\theta(1+\cos\theta)}$
$\because \sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1$
= $\lim_{\theta\to 0} \frac{\sin^2\theta}{\sin\theta(1+\cos\theta)} = \lim_{\theta\to 0} \frac{\sin\theta}{1+\cos\theta}$
= $\frac{\sin 0}{1+\cos 0} = \frac{0}{1+1} = \frac{0}{2} = 0$

Q.5 Express each limit in terms of e:
(i) $Lim_{n \to \infty} (1+\frac{1}{n})^{2n}$
$Lim_{n \to \infty} (1+\frac{1}{n})^{2n}$
$= Lim_{n \to \infty} [(1+\frac{1}{n})^n]^2 = [Lim_{n \to \infty} (1+\frac{1}{n})^n]^2 = e^2$

(ii) $Lim_{n \to \infty} (1+\frac{1}{n})^{\frac{n}{2}}$
$Lim_{n \to \infty} (1+\frac{1}{n})^{\frac{n}{2}}$
$= Lim_{n \to \infty} [(1+\frac{1}{n})^n]^{\frac{1}{2}} = [Lim_{n \to \infty} (1+\frac{1}{n})^n]^{\frac{1}{2}} = e^{\frac{1}{2}}$

(iii) $Lim_{n \to \infty} (1-\frac{1}{n})^n$
$Lim_{n \to \infty} (1-\frac{1}{n})^n = Lim_{n \to \infty} [(1+(-\frac{1}{n}))^{-n}]^{-1}$
$= [Lim_{n \to \infty} (1+(-\frac{1}{n}))^{-n}]^{-1} = e^{-1} = \frac{1}{e}$

(iv) $Lim_{n \to \infty} (1+\frac{1}{3n})^n$
$Lim_{n \to \infty} (1+\frac{1}{3n})^n$
$= Lim_{n \to \infty} [(1+\frac{1}{3n})^{3n}]^{\frac{1}{3}} = [Lim_{n \to \infty} (1+\frac{1}{3n})^{3n}]^{\frac{1}{3}}$
$Lim_{n \to \infty} (1+\frac{1}{3n})^n = e^{\frac{1}{3}}$

(v) $Lim_{n \to \infty} (1+\frac{4}{n})^n$
$Lim_{n \to \infty} (1+\frac{4}{n})^n = Lim_{n \to \infty} [(1+\frac{4}{n})^{\frac{n}{4}}]^4$
$= (Lim_{n \to \infty} (1+\frac{4}{n})^{\frac{n}{4}})^4 = e^4$

(vi) $Lim_{x \to 0} (1+3x)^{\frac{1}{x}}$
$Lim_{x \to 0} (1+3x)^{\frac{1}{x}} = Lim_{x \to 0} [(1+3x)^{\frac{1}{3x}}]^3$
$= [Lim_{x \to 0} (1+3x)^{\frac{1}{3x}}]^3 = e^3$

(vii) $Lim_{x \to 0} (1+2x^2)^{\frac{1}{x^2}}$
$Lim_{x \to 0} (1+2x^2)^{\frac{1}{x^2}} = lim_{x \to 0} [(1+2x^2)^{\frac{1}{2x^2}}]^2$
$[Lim_{x \to 0} (1+2x^2)^{\frac{1}{2x^2}}]^2 = e^2$

(viii) $lim_{x \to 0} \frac{e^{ax} - e^{bx}}{abx}$
We can rewrite the given expression as:
$= lim_{x \to 0} \frac{e^{ax} - 1 - e^{bx} + 1}{abx}$
$= lim_{x \to 0} \frac{(e^{ax} - 1) - (e^{bx} - 1)}{abx}$
$= lim_{x \to 0} (\frac{e^{ax} - 1}{abx} - \frac{e^{bx} - 1}{abx})$
$= lim_{x \to 0} \frac{e^{ax} - 1}{abx} - lim_{x \to 0} \frac{e^{bx} - 1}{abx}$
$= \frac{1}{b} (lim_{x \to 0} \frac{e^{ax} - 1}{ax}) - \frac{1}{a} (lim_{x \to 0} \frac{e^{bx} - 1}{bx})$
$= lim_{x \to 0} \frac{e^x - 1}{x} = 1$

--- Page 285 ---
$$=\frac{1}{b}(1)-\frac{1}{a}(1)=\frac{1}{b}-\frac{1}{a}=\frac{a-b}{ab}$$
$$\text{So, }\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{e^{ax}-e^{bx}}{abx}=\frac{a-b}{ab}$$

$$\text{(ix) }\quad Lim_{x\to\infty}\left(\frac{x}{1+x}\right)^x$$
$$\text{Solution:}$$
$$Lim_{x\to\infty}\left(\frac{x}{1+x}\right)^x=Lim_{x\to\infty}\left(\frac{1+x}{x}\right)^{-x}$$
$$=Lim_{x\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{x}+1\right)^{-x}=Lim_{x\to\infty}\left[\left(1+\frac{1}{x}\right)^x\right]^{-1}$$
$$=\left[Lim_{x\to\infty}\left(1+\frac{1}{x}\right)^x\right]^{-1}=e^{-1}=\frac{1}{e}$$

$$\text{(x) }\quad Lim_{x\to 0}\frac{e^{\frac{1}{x}}-1}{e^{\frac{1}{x}}+1},x<0$$
$$\text{Solution:}$$
$$\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{e^{\frac{1}{x}}-1}{e^{\frac{1}{x}}+1},x<0$$
$$\text{Let }t=e^{\frac{1}{x}}$$
$$\text{As }x<0,\text{ when }x\to 0,\text{ then }t\to 0.$$
$$\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{e^{\frac{1}{x}}-1}{e^{\frac{1}{x}}+1}=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{t-1}{t+1}=\frac{0-1}{0+1}=-1$$

$$\text{(xi) }\quad Lim_{x\to 0}\frac{e^{\frac{1}{x}}-1}{e^{\frac{1}{x}}+1},x>0$$
$$\text{Solution:}$$
$$\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{e^{\frac{1}{x}}-1}{e^{\frac{1}{x}}+1},x>0$$
$$\text{Let }t=e^{\frac{1}{x}},\text{ As }x>0,\text{ when }x\to 0\text{ then }$$
$$t\to\infty$$
$$\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{e^{\frac{1}{x}}-1}{e^{\frac{1}{x}}+1}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{t-1}{t+1}$$
$$=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{t\left(1-\frac{1}{t}\right)}{t\left(1+\frac{1}{t}\right)}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1-\frac{1}{t}}{1+\frac{1}{t}}=\frac{1-\frac{1}{\infty}}{1+\frac{1}{\infty}}=\frac{1-0}{1+0}=1$$

$$\text{(xii) }\quad \lim_{x\to 2}\frac{e^x-e^2}{x-2}$$
$$\text{Solution:}$$
$$\lim_{x\to 2}\frac{e^x-e^2}{x-2}$$
$$=\lim_{x\to 2}\frac{e^2\left(\frac{e^x}{e^2}-\frac{e^2}{e^2}\right)}{x-2}$$
$$=\lim_{x\to 2}\frac{e^2\left(e^{x-2}-1\right)}{x-2}$$
$$\text{Put }x-2=t$$
$$\text{As }x\to 2,t\to 0$$
$$=\lim_{t\to 0}e^2\left(\frac{e^t-1}{t}\right)$$
$$=e^2(1)$$
$$=e^2$$

Q.1 A substance decays exponentially
following the formula A(t) = A₀e⁻⁰.¹ᵗ,
where A₀ is the initial amount. Find
the limit of A(t) as t → ∞
A(t) = A₀e⁻⁰.¹ᵗ
Appling limit t → ∞ on both sides
lim A(t) = lim A₀e⁻⁰.¹ᵗ
t→∞      t→∞
= A₀ lim e⁻⁰.¹ᵗ
      t→∞
As t → ∞, e⁻⁰.¹ᵗ → 0
= A₀ × 0
lim A(t) = 0
t→∞

Q.2 A town's population is modeled by
P(t) = 100,000 / 1 + 9e⁻⁰.⁵ᵗ. What is the long-
term population as t → ∞.
The population of a town is given by:
P(t) = 100,000 / 1 + 9e⁻⁰.⁵ᵗ
Appling limit t → ∞ on both sides
lim P(t) = lim 100,000 / 1 + 9e⁻⁰.⁵ᵗ
t→∞      t→∞
As t → ∞, e⁻⁰.⁵ᵗ = 1 / e⁰.⁵ᵗ → 0
= lim 100,000 / 1 + 9(0) = 100,000 / 1 + 0 = 100,000 / 1
t→∞
lim P(t) = 100,000
t→∞
Thus, as the time 't' increases
indefinitely, the population of a town
becomes 100,000.

Q.3 A company's weekly sales (in
thousands) follow the function
S(t) = 500t / t + 10. What is the limit of
S(t) as t → ∞ and what does it
represent?
A company's weekly sales (in
thousands) is given by: S(t) = 500t / t + 10
Appling limit t → ∞ on both sides
lim S(t) = lim 500t / t + 10
t→∞      t→∞
= lim 500t / t(1 + 10/t)
  t→∞
= lim 500 / 1 + 10/t
  t→∞
As t → ∞, 10/t → 0
= lim 500 / 1 + 0
  t→∞
lim S(t) = 500
t→∞

Q.4 Signal strength S(d) at a distance d
from tower is modeled as
S(d) = 1000 / d²
(i) What is the signal at d = 10?
S(d) = 1000 / d²
We put d = 10
S(10) = 1000 / (10)² = 1000 / 100
S(10) = 10

--- Page 290 ---
[WANT TO GET ALL SUBJECT NOTES: WWW.FREEILM.COM]

(ii) What happens to signal strength as
$d \rightarrow \infty$?
$S(d) = \frac{1000}{d^2}$
Applying limit $d \rightarrow \infty$ on both sides
$\lim_{d \rightarrow \infty} S(d) = \lim_{d \rightarrow \infty} \frac{1000}{d^2}$
$= (1000) \lim_{d \rightarrow \infty} \frac{1}{d^2}$
As $d \rightarrow \infty, \frac{1}{d^2} \rightarrow 0$
$= 1000 \times 0$
$\lim_{d \rightarrow \infty} S(d) = 0$
When the distance '$d$' from the tower
increases indefinitely, the signal strength
$S(d)$ approaches to '0'

Q.5 A stock price grows according to the
function $P(t) = 50e^{0.05t}$
(i) Find the limit of $P(t)$ as $t \rightarrow \infty$.
Appling limit $t \rightarrow \infty$
$\lim_{t \rightarrow \infty} P(t) = \lim_{t \rightarrow \infty} 50e^{0.05t}$
$= 50 \lim_{t \rightarrow \infty} e^{0.05t}$
As $t \rightarrow \infty, e^{0.05t} \rightarrow \infty$
So, $\lim_{t \rightarrow \infty} P(t) = \infty$

(ii) Calculate the price after 10 years.
Put $t = 10$
$P(10) = 50e^{0.05(10)}$
$P(10) = 50e^{\frac{1}{2}}$
$= 82.436 \approx 82.44$

Q.6 The factory's cost function is given as:
$C(x) = \begin{cases} 10x + 500 \text{ if } x \le 100 \\ 12x + 300 \text{ if } x > 100 \end{cases}$
Is the cost function continuous at
$x = 100$?
At $x = 100$
$C(x) = 10x + 500$
$C(100) = 10(100) + 500$
$= 1000 + 500 = 1500$

[WEBSITE: WWW.FREEILM.COM]

$\lim_{x \rightarrow 100^-} C(x) = \lim_{x \rightarrow 100^-} (10x + 500)$
$\lim_{x \rightarrow 100^-} C(x) = 10(100) + 500$
$\lim_{x \rightarrow 100^-} C(x) = 1000 + 500 = 1500$
$\lim_{x \rightarrow 100^+} C(x) = \lim_{x \rightarrow 100^+} (12x + 300)$
$= 12(100) + 300$
$\lim_{x \rightarrow 100^+} C(x) = 1200 + 300 = 1500$
As $\lim_{x \rightarrow 100^-} C(x) = \lim_{x \rightarrow 100^+} C(x)$, So
$\lim_{x \rightarrow 100} C(x)$ exists and equal to 1500
As $C(100) = \lim_{x \rightarrow 100} C(x)$
Hence, $C(x)$ is Continuous at $x = 100$

Q.7 Inflation is modeled by $I(t) = I_0 e^{0.03t}$,
where $I_0$ is the initial price index and
$t$ is the number of years.
(i) Find the inflation rate after 5 years if
$I_0 = 100$
$I(t) = I_0 e^{0.03t}$
Put $I_0 = 100$, $t = 5$
we get,
$I(5) = 100e^{0.03(5)}$
$I(5) = 100e^{0.15}$
$I(5) \approx 116.18$
$\because \text{Percentage increase} = \frac{\text{Net increase}}{\text{Original value}} \times 100\%$
Now, to find inflation rate
$\text{Inflation rate} = \frac{I(t) - I_0}{I_0} \times 100\%$
$= \frac{I(5) - 100}{100} \times 100\%$
$\approx \frac{116.18 - 100}{100} \times 100\%$
$\text{Inflation rate} \approx 16.18\%$

(ii) What is the expected price index after
10 years?
Put $I_o = 100, t = 10$
$I(t) = I_o e^{0.03t}$
$I(10) = 100e^{0.03(10)}$
$I(10) = 100e^{0.3}$
$I(10) \approx 134.99$
Q.8 The cost to produce $x$ units is:
$C(x) = \begin{cases} 5x + 20 & \text{if} & x \le 10 \\ 6x + 10 & \text{if} & x > 10 \end{cases}$
Is the cost function continuous at
$x = 10$?
At $x = 10$
$C(x) = 5x + 20$
$C(10) = 5(10) + 20$
$= 50 + 20$
$C(10) = 70$
$\lim_{x \to 10^-} C(x) = \lim_{x \to 10^-} (5x + 20)$
$= 5(10) + 20 = 50 + 20 = 70$
$\lim_{x \to 10^+} C(x) = \lim_{x \to 10^+} (6x + 10)$
$= 6(10) + 10 = 60 + 10 = 70$
As $\lim_{x \to 10^-} C(x) = \lim_{x \to 10^+} C(x)$, So
$\lim_{x \to 10} C(x)$ exists and equal to 70
As $C(10) = \lim_{x \to 10} C(x)$
Hence, $C(x)$ is continuous at $x = 10$

$\delta y = \delta x \left[ \frac{-1}{\sqrt{x}.\sqrt{x+\delta x} \left[ \sqrt{x} + \sqrt{x+\delta x} \right]} \right]$
Dividing by $\delta x$ on both sides
$\frac{\delta y}{\delta x} = \frac{\delta x}{\delta x} \left[ \frac{-1}{\sqrt{x}.\sqrt{x+\delta x} \left[ \sqrt{x} + \sqrt{x+\delta x} \right]} \right]$
$\lim_{\delta x \to 0} \frac{\delta y}{\delta x} = \lim_{\delta x \to 0} \left[ \frac{-1}{\sqrt{x}.\sqrt{x+\delta x} \left[ \sqrt{x} + \sqrt{x+\delta x} \right]} \right]$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{x}.\sqrt{x+0} \left[ \sqrt{x} + \sqrt{x+0} \right]}$
$\frac{d}{dx} \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{x}} \right) = \frac{-1}{x(2\sqrt{x})}$
$\because a^m.a^n = a^{m+n}$
$\frac{d}{dx} \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{x}} \right) = \frac{-1}{2x^{\frac{3}{2}}}$
(iv) $y = x(x-3)$
Let $y = x(x-3)$
$y = x^2 - 3x$
$y + \delta y = (x+\delta x)^2 - 3(x+\delta x)$
$\delta y = x^2 + (\delta x)^2 + 2x\delta x - 3x - 3\delta x - y$
$\delta y = x^2 + (\delta x)^2 + 2x\delta x - 3x - 3\delta x + 3x - x^2$
$\delta y = \delta x [\delta x + 2x - 3]$
Dividing by $\delta x$
$\frac{\delta y}{\delta x} = \frac{\delta x}{\delta x} [\delta x + 2x - 3]$
Applying $\lim_{\delta x \to 0}$ on both sides
$\lim_{\delta x \to 0} \frac{\delta y}{\delta x} = \lim_{\delta x \to 0} (\delta x + 2x - 3)$
$\because \lim_{\delta x \to 0} \frac{\delta y}{\delta x} = \frac{dy}{dx}$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = 0 + 2x - 3$
$\frac{d}{dx} [x(x-3)] = 2x - 3$

2. Find $\frac{dy}{dx}$ from first principle and find
gradient of curve at the given point.
(i) $\sqrt{x+2}$ at $x = 6$
Let $y = \sqrt{x+2}$
$y + \delta y = \sqrt{x+\delta x+2}$
$\delta y = \sqrt{x+\delta x+2} - y$
$\delta y = \sqrt{x+\delta x+2} - \sqrt{x+2}$
By rationalizing, we get
$\delta y = \sqrt{x+\delta x+2} - \sqrt{x+2} \left[ \frac{\sqrt{x+\delta x+2} + \sqrt{x+2}}{\sqrt{x+\delta x+2} + \sqrt{x+2}} \right]$
$\delta y = \frac{(\sqrt{x+\delta x+2})^2 - (\sqrt{x+2})^2}{\sqrt{x+\delta x+2} + \sqrt{x+2}}$
$\delta y = \frac{x+\delta x+2 - x - 2}{\sqrt{x+\delta x+2} + \sqrt{x+2}}$
$\delta y = \delta x \left[ \frac{1}{\sqrt{x+\delta x+2} + \sqrt{x+2}} \right]$
$\frac{\delta y}{\delta x} = \frac{\delta x}{\delta x} \left[ \frac{1}{\sqrt{x+\delta x+2} + \sqrt{x+2}} \right]$
$\lim_{\delta x \to 0} \frac{\delta y}{\delta x} = \lim_{\delta x \to 0} \frac{1}{\sqrt{x+\delta x+2} + \sqrt{x+2}}$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{x+0+2} + \sqrt{x+2}}$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x+2}}$
At $x = 6$
$\frac{dy}{dx} \Big|_{x=6} = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{6+2}}$
$\frac{dy}{dx} \Big|_{x=6} = \frac{1}{4\sqrt{2}}$

(ii) Let $y = \frac{1}{\sqrt{x+a}}$ at $x=a$
$y = \frac{1}{\sqrt{x+a}}$
$y + \delta y = \frac{1}{\sqrt{x+a+\delta x}}$
$\delta y = \frac{1}{x+a+\delta x} - y$
$\delta y = \frac{1}{\sqrt{x+a+\delta x}} - \frac{1}{\sqrt{x+a}}$
$\delta y = \frac{\sqrt{x+a} - \sqrt{x+a+\delta x}}{\sqrt{x+a+\delta x}.\sqrt{x+a}}$
Rationalizing by numerator
$\delta y = \frac{\sqrt{x+a} - \sqrt{x+a+\delta x}}{\sqrt{x+a+\delta x}.\sqrt{x+a}} \left[ \frac{\sqrt{x+a} + \sqrt{x+a+\delta x}}{\sqrt{x+a} + \sqrt{x+a+\delta x}} \right]$
$\delta y = \frac{(\sqrt{x+a})^2 - (\sqrt{x+a+\delta x})^2}{(\sqrt{x+a+\delta x}.\sqrt{x+a})(\sqrt{x+a} + \sqrt{x+a+\delta x})}$
$\delta y = \frac{x+a - x - a - \delta x}{(\sqrt{x+a+\delta x}.\sqrt{x+a})(\sqrt{x+a} + \sqrt{x+a+\delta x})}$
$\delta y = \delta x \left[ \frac{-1}{(\sqrt{x+a+\delta x}.\sqrt{x+a})(\sqrt{x+a} + \sqrt{x+a+\delta x})} \right]$
$\frac{\delta y}{\delta x} = \frac{\delta x}{\delta x} \left[ \frac{-1}{\sqrt{x+a+\delta x}.\sqrt{x+a}(\sqrt{x+a} + \sqrt{x+a+\delta x})} \right]$
$\lim_{\delta x \to 0} \frac{\delta y}{\delta x} = \lim_{\delta x \to 0} \left[ \frac{-1}{(\sqrt{x+a+\delta x}\sqrt{x+a})(\sqrt{x+a} + \sqrt{x+a+\delta x})} \right]$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{-1}{\sqrt{x+a+0}\sqrt{x+a}(\sqrt{x+a} + 0 + \sqrt{x+a})}$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{-1}{(x+a)[2\sqrt{x+a}]}$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{-1}{2(x+a)^{\frac{3}{2}}}$
At $x=a$
$\frac{dy}{dx}|_{x=a} = \frac{-1}{2(a+a)^{\frac{3}{2}}}$
$\frac{dy}{dx}|_{x=a} = \frac{-1}{2(2a)^{\frac{3}{2}}}$
Gradient = $\frac{-1}{2^{\frac{5}{2}}.a^{\frac{3}{2}}}$

(i) Find the derivative of $x^{\frac{2}{3}}$ at
$x=8$ from the first principle.
Let $y = x^{\frac{2}{3}}$
$y + \delta y = (x+\delta x)^{\frac{2}{3}}$
$\delta y = (x+\delta x)^{\frac{2}{3}} - y$
$\delta y = (x+\delta x)^{\frac{2}{3}} - x^{\frac{2}{3}}$
$\delta y = x^{\frac{2}{3}} \left[ 1 + \frac{\delta x}{x} \right]^{\frac{2}{3}} - x^{\frac{2}{3}}$
By using binomial series expansion
$\because (1+x)^n = 1 + nx + \text{involving terms } x...)$
$\delta y = x^{\frac{2}{3}} \left[ 1 + \frac{2}{3} \left( \frac{\delta x}{x} \right) + \text{involving terms } \left( \frac{\delta x}{x} \right) - 1 \right]$
$\delta y = x^{\frac{2}{3}} \left[ \frac{2}{3} \frac{1}{x} + \text{involving terms } \left( \frac{\delta x}{x} \right) \right]$
$\frac{\delta y}{\delta x} = \frac{x^{\frac{2}{3}}}{\delta x} \left[ \frac{2}{3} \frac{1}{x} + \text{involving terms } \frac{\delta x}{x} \right]$
$\lim_{\delta x \to 0} \frac{\delta y}{\delta x} = \lim_{\delta x \to 0} x^{\frac{2}{3}} \left[ \frac{2}{3} \frac{1}{x} + \text{involving terms } \frac{\delta x}{x} \right]$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = x^{\frac{2}{3}} \left[ \frac{2}{3x} + 0 + 0 + ... + 0 ... \right]$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{2}{3} x^{\frac{2}{3}-1}$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{2}{3} x^{-\frac{1}{3}}$
At $x=8$
$\frac{dy}{dx}|_{x=8} = \frac{2}{3} (8)^{-\frac{1}{3}}$

(ii) Find the derivative of $x^2 + 2x + 3$ by
definition.
Let $y = x^2 + 2x + 3$
$y + \delta y = (x + \delta x)^2 + 2(x + \delta x) + 3$
$\delta y = x^2 + (\delta x)^2 + 2x\delta x + 2x + 2\delta x + 3 - y$
$\delta y = x^2 + (\delta x)^2 + 2x\delta x + 2x + 3 - x^2 - 2x - 3 + 2x$
$\delta y = \delta x [\delta x + 2x + 2]$
$\frac{\delta y}{\delta x} = \frac{\delta x}{\delta x} [\delta x + 2x + 2]$
$\lim_{\delta x \to 0} \frac{\delta y}{\delta x} = \lim_{\delta x \to 0} [\delta x + 2x + 2]$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = 0 + 2x + 2$
$\frac{d}{dx}(y) = 2x + 2$
$\frac{d}{dx}(x^2 + 2x + 3) = 2x + 2$

4. Find from first principle, the
derivatives of the following
expressions w.r.t their respective
independent variables:
(i) $(3x-2)^{-2}$
Let $y = (3x-2)^{-2}$
$y + \delta y = [3(x + \delta x) - 2]^{-2}$
$\delta y = (3x + 3\delta x - 2)^{-2} - y$
$\delta y = (3x - 2 + 3\delta x)^{-2} - (3x - 2)^{-2}$
$\delta y = (3x - 2)^{-2} \left[ 1 + \frac{3\delta x}{3x - 2} \right]^{-2} - (3x - 2)^{-2}$
$\delta y = (3x - 2)^{-2} \left[ \left( 1 + \frac{3\delta x}{3x - 2} \right)^2 - 1 \right]$
By using Binomial series expansion
$(1+x)^n = 1 + nx + \frac{n(n-1)}{2!}x^2 + \frac{n(n-1)(n-2)}{3!}x^3 + \dots$
$(1+x)^n = 1 + nx + \text{involving terms } x$
$\delta y = (3x - 2)^{-2} \left[ 1 - 2 \frac{3\delta x}{3x - 2} + \text{involving terms} - 1 \right]$
$\delta y = (3x - 2)^{-2} \delta x \left[ \frac{-6}{3x - 2} + \text{involving terms } \delta x \right]$
$\frac{\delta y}{\delta x} = \frac{(3x - 2)^{-2} \delta x}{\delta x} \left[ \frac{-6}{3x - 2} + \text{involving terms } \delta x \right]$
$\lim_{\delta x \to 0} \frac{\delta y}{\delta x} = \lim_{\delta x \to 0} (3x - 2)^{-2} \left[ \frac{-6}{3x - 2} + \text{involving terms } \delta x \right]$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = (3x - 2)^{-2} \left[ \frac{-6}{3x - 2} + 0 + 0 + \dots \right]$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = -6(3x - 2)^{-3}$
$\frac{d}{dx}(y) = -6(3x - 2)^{-3}$
$\frac{d}{dx}(3x - 2)^{-2} = -6(3x - 2)^{-3}$

(ii) $y = (2t + 3)^5$

$y + \delta y = [2(t + \delta t) + 3]^5$
$\delta y = (2t + 3 + 2\delta t)^5 - y$
$\delta y = [(2t + 3) + 2\delta t]^5 - (2t + 3)^5$
By using Binomial Theorem i-e
$(a+b)^n = a^n + n a^{n-1} b + \frac{n(n-1)}{2!} a^{n-2} b^2 + \dots + b^n$
$\delta y = (2t + 3)^5 + 5(2t + 3)^4 (2\delta t) + \dots$
$+ (2\delta t)^5 - (2t + 3)^5$

5. Find the gradient and equation of
tangent line to $y = 3x^2 - 4x + 1$ at
$x = 2$
Gradient $= m \Rightarrow \lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x) - f(a)}{x - a}$
$m = \lim_{x \to 2} \frac{f(x) - f(2)}{x - 2}$
$m = \lim_{x \to 2} \left[ \frac{3x^2 - 4x + 1 - (3(2)^2 - 4(2) + 1)}{x - 2} \right]$
$m = \lim_{x \to 2} \left[ \frac{3x^2 - 4x + 1 - 5}{x - 2} \right]$
$m = \lim_{x \to 2} \left( \frac{3x^2 - 4x - 4}{x - 2} \right)$
$m = \lim_{x \to 2} \left( \frac{3x^2 - 6x + 2x - 4}{x - 2} \right)$
$m = \lim_{x \to 2} \frac{(3x+2)(x-2)}{(x-2)}$
$m = 3(2) + 2$
$m = 8$
Hence gradient = 8
If $x = 2$ then $y = 3(2)^2 - 4(2) + 1$
$y = 12 - 8 + 1$
$y = 5$
So point is $(2, 5) = (x_1, y_1)$
Equation of tangent is at $(2, 5)$
$y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)$
$y - 5 = 8(x - 2)$
$y - 5 = 8x - 16$
$8x - y - 11 = 0$
$y = 8x - 11$

12. The outdoor temperature (in °C)

rate of change at t = 2.

Instantaneous Rate of change
= lim T(t) - T(a)
t→a t - a
lim f(x) - f(a) = f'(a)
x→a x - a
T'(a) = lim T(t) - T(a)
t→a t - a
T(t) = -t² + 12t + 10
d [T(t)] = - d t² + 12 d (t) + d (10)
dt dt dt dt
T'(t) = -2t + 12

T'(2) = 8

Theorems on differentiation:
(1) $\frac{d}{dx}(c)=0$                                     ($c$ is constant)
(2) $\frac{d}{dx}(x^n)=nx^{n-1}$,                      $n \in R$ (The Power rule)
(3) $\frac{d}{dx}[cf(x)]=c.f'(x)$
(4) $\frac{d}{dx}(f(x)+g(x))=f'(x)+g'(x)$
(5) The Product Rule:
    $\frac{d}{dx}[f(x)g(x)]=f'(x)g(x)+f(x)g'(x)$
(6) The Quotient Rule:
    $\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}\right)=\frac{f'(x)g(x)-f(x)g'(x)}{[g(x)]^2}, g(x) \neq 0$

$y = \frac{x - x^2 + x^2 - x^2}{\sqrt{x}}$
$\because a^m . a^n = a^{m+n}$

$\because \frac{a^m}{a^n} = a^{m-n}$
$y = \sqrt{x} - x^{2 - \frac{1}{2}}$
$y = x^{\frac{1}{2}} - x^{\frac{3}{2}}$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{d}{dx} x^{\frac{1}{2}} - \frac{d}{dx} x^{\frac{3}{2}}$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{2} x^{\frac{1}{2} - 1} - \frac{3}{2} x^{\frac{3}{2} - 1}$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{2} x^{-\frac{1}{2}} - \frac{3}{2} x^{\frac{1}{2}}$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}} - \frac{3}{2} \sqrt{x}$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1 - 3x}{2\sqrt{x}}$

(v) $y = \left( \sqrt{x} - \frac{1}{\sqrt{x}} \right)^2$

$\because x^{\frac{3}{2}} = x.\sqrt{x}$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = 1 + 2x^{-3} + x^{-\frac{3}{2}}$

$\frac{dy}{dx} = (x - 5)(0 - 1) + (3 - x)(1 - 0)$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = -x + 5 + 3 - x$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = 8 - 2x$

(vii) $y = \frac{(x^2 + 1)^2}{x^2 - 1}$

$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{(x^2 - 1) \frac{d}{dx} (x^2 + 1)^2 - (x^2 + 1)^2 \frac{d}{dx} (x^2 - 1)}{(x^2 - 1)^2}$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{(x^2 - 1) 2(x^2 + 1).2x - (x^2 + 1)^2 . (2x)}{(x^2 - 1)^2}$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{2x(x^2 + 1) [2x^2 - 2 - x^2 - 1]}{(x^2 - 1)^2}$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{2x(x^2 + 1)(x^2 - 3)}{(x^2 - 1)^2}$
$\because (\frac{u}{v})' = \frac{vu' - uv'}{v^2}$
$\because \frac{d}{dx} [f(x)]^n = n.[f(x)]^{n-1} . f'(x)$

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{\sqrt{x^2-1}}{2\sqrt{x^2+1}} \cdot \frac{(x^2-1)(x^2+1)' - (x^2+1)(x^2-1)'}{(x^2-1)^2}$$

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{\sqrt{x^2-1}}{2\sqrt{x^2+1}} \cdot \frac{(x^2-1)(2x) - (x^2+1)2x}{(x^2-1)^2}$$

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{2x(x^2-1-x^2-1).\sqrt{x^2-1}}{2\sqrt{x^2+1}.(x^2-1)^2}$$

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{-2x}{\sqrt{x^2+1}(x^2-1)^{\frac{3}{2}}}$$

**2. Find $\frac{dy}{dx}$ if $y = \frac{(\sqrt{x}+1)\left(x^{\frac{3}{2}}-1\right)}{\sqrt{x}-1}, x \neq 1$**

**Solution:**

$$y = \frac{(\sqrt{x}+1)\left(x^{\frac{3}{2}}-1\right)}{\sqrt{x}-1} \quad \because x \neq 1$$

$$= \frac{(\sqrt{x}+1)\left[(\sqrt{x})^3 - (1)^3\right]}{\sqrt{x}-1}$$

$$= \frac{(\sqrt{x}+1)(\sqrt{x}-1)(x+1+\sqrt{x})}{\sqrt{x}-1}$$

$$= (\sqrt{x}+1)(x+1+\sqrt{x})$$

$$= (\sqrt{x}+1)(\sqrt{x}+1+x)$$

$$= (\sqrt{x}+1)^2 + (\sqrt{x}+1)x$$

$$= x+1+2\sqrt{x} + x\sqrt{x}+x$$

$$= x^{\frac{3}{2}} + 2x + 2x^{\frac{1}{2}} + 1$$

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{d}{dx}\left(x^{\frac{3}{2}} + 2x + 2x^{\frac{1}{2}} + 1\right)$$

$$= \frac{d}{dx}\left(x^{\frac{3}{2}}\right) + \frac{d}{dx}(2x) + \frac{d}{dx}\left(2x^{\frac{1}{2}}\right) + \frac{d}{dx}(1)$$

$$= \frac{3}{2}x^{\frac{1}{2}} + 2(1) + 2.\frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}} + 0$$

$$= \frac{3}{2}\sqrt{x} + 2 + \frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}$$

**(Alternative)**

**Find $\frac{dy}{dx}$ if $y = \frac{(\sqrt{x}+1)\left(x^{\frac{3}{2}}-1\right)}{\sqrt{x}-1}, x \neq 1$**

**Solution:**
First of all simplify the function

$$y = \frac{(\sqrt{x}+1)\left[\left(x^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^3 - 1\right]}{\sqrt{x}-1}$$

$$\because a^3 - b^3 = (a-b)(a^2+ab+b^2)$$

$$y = \frac{(\sqrt{x}+1)\left[(\sqrt{x}-1)(x+\sqrt{x}+1)\right]}{(\sqrt{x}-1)}$$

$$y = (\sqrt{x}+1)(x+\sqrt{x}+1)$$

$$y = x^{\frac{3}{2}} + x + \sqrt{x} + x + \sqrt{x} + 1$$

$$y = x^{\frac{3}{2}} + 2\sqrt{x} + 2x + 1$$


$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{d}{dx}x^{\frac{3}{2}} + 2\frac{d}{dx}\sqrt{x} + 2\frac{d}{dx}x + \frac{d}{dx}(1)$$

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{3}{2}x^{\frac{3}{2}-1} + 2.\frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}} + 2 + 0$$

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{3\sqrt{x}}{2} + \frac{1}{\sqrt{x}} + 2 = \frac{3x+2+4\sqrt{x}}{2\sqrt{x}}$$

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{3x+2+4\sqrt{x}}{2\sqrt{x}}$$

**3. Differentiate $\frac{(\sqrt{x}+1)\left(x^{\frac{3}{2}}-1\right)}{x^{\frac{3}{2}}-x^{\frac{1}{2}}}$ with respect to $x$**

**Solution:**

Let $y = \frac{(\sqrt{x}+1)\left(x^{\frac{3}{2}}-1\right)}{x^{\frac{3}{2}}-x^{\frac{1}{2}}}$

$$= \frac{(\sqrt{x}+1)\left((\sqrt{x})^3-1\right)}{\sqrt{x}(x-1)}$$

$$= \frac{(\sqrt{x}+1)(\sqrt{x}-1)(x+\sqrt{x}+1)}{\sqrt{x}(x-1)}$$

5. The position of a particle moving along
a line is given by $s(t)=5t^3-12t^2+8t$,

$t=1.13\text{ sec}$ or $t=0.47\text{ sec}$

Q.1 Let u = 3i + 2j - 5k, v = i - 5j - k
and w = -4i - j + 7k. Find the
following.
(i) u + 2v + w
u + 2v + w =
3i + 2j - 5k + 2(i - 5j - k)
+(-4i - j + 7k)
= i - 9j
(ii) v - 3w
v - 3w = i - 5j - k - 3(-4i - j + 7k)
= i - 5j - k + 12i + 3j - 21k
= 13i - 2j - 22k
(iii) |3v + w|
|3v + w|
= |3(i - 5j - k) + (-4i - j + 7k)|
= |3i - 15j - 3k - 4i - j + 7k|
= |-i - 16j + 4k|
= √(-1)² + (-16)² + (4)²
= √273

Q.3 Find t so that
|2i + (t - 1)j + tk| = √13
We have |2i + (t - 1)j + tk| = √13
⇒ √(2)² + (t - 1)² + (t)² = √13
⇒ √4 + t² - 2t + 1 + t² = √13
Squaring both sides, we have
2t² - 2t + 5 = 13
⇒ 2t² - 2t - 8 = 0
Gives t² - t - 4 = 0
Using quadratic formula, we have
t = (-1) ± √(-1)² - 4(1)(-4) = 1 ± √17
2(1) 2

Q.5 If u = 2i + j - 3k, v = -i + 4j + 2k

Q.7 If u = xi + 2j + 3k, v = i + yj - 3k
and w = -2i - 3j represent the sides
of a triangle, find the values of x and y.

and w = -2i - 3j represent the sides
of a triangle then we must have
u + v = w
i.e; (x + 1)i + (2 + y)j = -2i - 3j
equating co-efficients of i and j, we
have x + 1 = -2 ⇒ x = -3 and
2 + y = -3 ⇒ y = -5
Q.8 The position vectors of points A, B, C
and D are

respectively. Show that AB is
parallel to CD
Here we have
AB = OB - OA = v - u
= (7i + 8j + 4k) - (i + 2j + k)
So AB = 6i + 6j + 3k and similarly
CD = OD - OC = z - w
= (i + 2j + 2k) - (-i + k)
CD = 2i + 2j + k
AB and CD will be parallel if
AB = λCD (λ ≠ 0)

So we have $\overline{AB} = 6\underline{i} + 6\underline{j} + 3\underline{k}$
$\overline{AB} = 3(2\underline{i} + 2\underline{j} + \underline{k})$
Gives $\overline{AB} = 3\overline{CD}$
Which shows that $\overline{AB}$ is parallel to $\overline{CD}$.
Q.9 We say that two vectors $\underline{v}$ and $\underline{w}$ in
space are parallel if there is a scalar $c$
such that $\underline{v} = c\underline{w}$ the vectors point in
the same direction if $c > 0$, and the
vectors point in the opposite direction
if $c < 0$
(a) Find two vectors of length 2
parallel to the vector
$\underline{v} = 2\underline{i} - 4\underline{j} + 4\underline{k}$
$\underline{v} = 2\underline{i} - 4\underline{j} + 4\underline{k}$
$|\underline{v}| = \sqrt{2^2 + (-4)^2 + (4)^2} = \sqrt{36} = 6$
If it is unit vector parallel to $\underline{v}$ then,
$\underline{\hat{v}} = \frac{\underline{v}}{|\underline{v}|} = \frac{2\underline{i} - 4\underline{j} + 4\underline{k}}{6}$
$= \frac{2}{6}\underline{i} - \frac{4}{6}\underline{j} + \frac{4}{6}\underline{k} = \frac{1}{3}\underline{i} - \frac{2}{3}\underline{j} + \frac{2}{3}\underline{k}$
The two vectors of length 2 and parallel
to $\underline{\hat{v}}$ are $2\underline{\hat{v}}$ and $-2\underline{\hat{v}}$
$2\underline{\hat{v}} = 2\left(\frac{1}{3}\underline{i} - \frac{2}{3}\underline{j} + \frac{2}{3}\underline{k}\right)$
and $-2\underline{\hat{v}} = -2\left(\frac{1}{3}\underline{i} - \frac{2}{3}\underline{j} + \frac{2}{3}\underline{k}\right)$
$2\underline{\hat{v}} = \frac{2}{3}\underline{i} - \frac{4}{3}\underline{j} + \frac{4}{3}\underline{k}$
and $-2\underline{\hat{v}} = -\frac{2}{3}\underline{i} + \frac{4}{3}\underline{j} - \frac{4}{3}\underline{k}$
(b) Find the constant $\alpha$ so that
the vectors $\underline{v} = \underline{i} - 3\underline{j} + 4\underline{k}$ and
$\underline{w} = \alpha\underline{i} + 9\underline{j} - 12\underline{k}$ are parallel.
If $\underline{v}$ and $\underline{w}$ are parallel then, $\underline{v} = c\underline{w}$
$\underline{i} - 3\underline{j} + 4\underline{k} = c(\alpha\underline{i} + 9\underline{j} - 12\underline{k})$
$\underline{i} - 3\underline{j} + 4\underline{k} = c\alpha\underline{i} + 9c\underline{j} - 12c\underline{k}$
Comparing both side
$ac = 1 \dots(i), 9c = -3$
$c = -\frac{1}{3}$, Put in (i), $\alpha = \left(-\frac{1}{3}\right) = 1$
$\alpha = -3$

(c) Find a vector of length 5 in the
direction opposite that of
$\underline{v} = \underline{i} - 2\underline{j} + 3\underline{k}$.
$\underline{v} = \underline{i} - 2\underline{j} + 3\underline{k}$
$|\underline{v}| = \sqrt{(1)^2 + (-2)^2 + (3)^2} = \sqrt{1 + 4 + 9} = \sqrt{14}$
If $\underline{\hat{v}}$ be unit vector in the direction
of $\underline{v}$ then $\underline{\hat{v}} = \frac{\underline{v}}{|\underline{v}|} = \frac{\underline{i} - 2\underline{j} + 3\underline{k}}{\sqrt{14}}$
Thus the required vector of length 5 and
direction opposite is:
$-5\underline{\hat{v}} = -5\left(\frac{\underline{i} - 2\underline{j} + 3\underline{k}}{\sqrt{14}}\right)$
$= \frac{-5}{\sqrt{14}}\underline{i} + \frac{10}{\sqrt{14}}\underline{j} - \frac{15}{\sqrt{14}}\underline{k}$
(d) Find $a$ and $b$ so that the vectors
$3\underline{i} - \underline{j} + 4\underline{k}$ and $a\underline{i} + b\underline{j} - 2\underline{k}$ are
Parallel.
Let $\underline{v} = 3\underline{i} - \underline{j} + 4\underline{k}$ and $\underline{w} = a\underline{i} + b\underline{j} - 2\underline{k}$
$\underline{v}$ and $\underline{w}$ are parallel if $\underline{w} = c\underline{v}$
$a\underline{i} + b\underline{j} - 2\underline{k} = c(3\underline{i} - \underline{j} + 4\underline{k})$
$a\underline{i} + b\underline{j} - 2\underline{k} = 3c\underline{i} - c\underline{j} + 4c\underline{k}$
Comparing both sides
$a = 3c \dots(i), b = -c \dots(ii),$
$-2 = 4c \dots(iii) \Rightarrow c = -\frac{1}{2}$
Put value of $c$ in equation (i) and (ii),
we have
$a = 3\left(\frac{-1}{2}\right)$ and $b = -\left(\frac{-1}{2}\right)$
$a = \frac{-3}{2}$ and $b = \frac{1}{2}$

Q.10 A spacecraft moves from point

|AB| = 10√179 kilometers

Q.12 Which of the following triples can be
the direction angles of a single vector:
(i) 45°,45°,60°
45°,45°,60°
Let α = 45°, β = 45°, γ = 60°
We are to show that
cos² α + cos² β + cos² γ = 1
L.H.S
= cos²(45) + cos²(45) + cos²(60)
= (1/√2)² + (1/√2)² + (1/2)² = 1/2 + 1/2 + 1/4
= 2+2+1 / 4 = 5/4 ≠ 1
Thus the given angles are not direction
angles of a vector.
(ii) 30°,45°,60°
Let α = 30°, β = 45°, γ = 60°
We are to show that
cos² α + cos² β + cos² γ = 1
L.H.S =
cos²(30°) + cos²(45°) + cos²(60°)
= (√3/2)² + (1/√2)² + (1/2)² = 3/4 + 1/2 + 1/4
= 3+2+1 / 4 = 6/4 ≠ 1
Thus the given angles are not direction
angle of the vector.
(iii) 45°,60°,60°
Let α = 45°, β = 60°, γ = 60°
We are to show that
cos² α + cos² β + cos² γ = 1
L.H.S
= cos²(45°) + cos²(60°) + cos²(60°)
= (1/√2)² + (1/2)² + (1/2)² = 1/2 + 1/4 + 1/4
= 2+1+1 / 4 = 4/4 = 1
Thus given angles are direction angles
of a single vector.

Q.1 Find the cosines of the angle θ
between u and v
(i) u = 2i + 3j + k, v = -i + 2j + 2k
u = 2i + 3j + k, v = -i + 2j + 2k
cos θ = u.v / |u||v|
cos θ = (2i + 3j + k).(-i + 2j + 2k) / √(2)² + (3)² + (1)² √(-1)² + (2)² + (2)²
= (2)(-1) + (3)(2) + (1)(2) / √4 + 9 + 1 √1 + 4 + 4
= 6 / 3√14, cos θ = 2 / √14 = √14 / 7
(ii) u = [-3, 2, 5], v = [1, 6, -2]
u = -3i + 2j + 5k, v = i + 6j - 2k
cos θ = u.v / |u||v|
cos θ = (-3i + 2j + 5j). (i + 6j - 2k) / √(-3)² + (2)² + (5)² √(1)² + (6)² + (-2)²
= -3 + 12 - 10 / √9 + 4 + 25 √1 + 36 + 4 = -1 / √38 × 41
cos θ = -1 / √1558
Q.2 If a + b + c = 0 and |a| = 3, |b| = 5
and |c| = 7 Find the angle between a
and b
Given that a + b + c = 0, so we have
a + b = -c
Squaring both sides, we get
(a + b)² = (-c)²
|a|² + |b|² + 2|a||b|cos θ = |c|² where
θ is the angle between them. Putting
given values, we have
⇒ (3)² + (5)² + 2(3)(5)cos θ = (7)²
⇒ 9 + 25 + 30 cos θ = 49
⇒ 30 cos θ = 49 - 9 - 25
⇒ 30 cos θ = 15

So we have cos θ = 15 / 30
Gives cos θ = 1/2 ⇒ θ = 60° or
⇒ θ = π / 3
Q.3 If |a| = 3, |b| = 4 and |a + b| = 5 Find
the angle between a and b
Here we have |a + b| = 5 and suppose
θ is the angle between a and b then
squaring the above result, we get
|a + b|² = (5)²
⇒ |a|² + |b|² + 2a.b = 25
⇒ |a|² + |b|² + 2|a||b|cos θ = 25
Putting given values, we have
(3)² + (4)² + 2(3)(4)cos θ = 25
⇒ 9 + 16 + 24 cos θ = 25
⇒ 24 cos θ = 25 - 25
⇒ 24 cos θ = 0 ⇒ cos θ = 0
Gives θ = 90° or θ = π / 2
Or we can say a ⊥ b
Q.4 Calculate the projection of a along b
and projection of b along a when
(i) a = 2i + 3j - k, b = i - 2j + 4k
a = 2i + 3j - k ⇒ |a| = √4 + 9 + 1 = √14
b = i - 2j + 4k ⇒ |b| = √1 + 4 + 16
= √21
Projection of a along b = a.b / |b|
= (2i + 3j - k).(i - 2j + 4k) / √21 = 2 - 6 - 4 / √21
= -8 / √21
Projection of b along a = a.b / |a| = -8 / √14

--- Page 313 ---
(ii) $\underline{a}=4\underline{i}-2\underline{j}+3\underline{k}, \underline{b}=\underline{i}+\underline{j}+\underline{k}$
$\underline{a}=4\underline{i}-2\underline{j}+3\underline{k}$
$|\underline{a}|=\sqrt{(4)^2+(-2)^2+(3)^2}=\sqrt{29}$
$\underline{b}=\underline{i}+\underline{j}+\underline{k}$
$|\underline{b}|=\sqrt{(1)^2+(1)^2+(1)^2}=\sqrt{3}$
Projection of $\underline{a}$ along $\underline{b}=\frac{\underline{a}.\underline{b}}{|\underline{b}|}$
$=\frac{(4\underline{i}-2\underline{j}+3\underline{k}).(\underline{i}+\underline{j}+\underline{k})}{\sqrt{3}}$
$=\frac{(4)(1)+(-2)(1)+(3)(1)}{\sqrt{3}}=\frac{5}{\sqrt{3}}$
